Stochastic homogenization on perforated domains I - Extension operators
Martin Heida

Abstract
In this first part of a series of 3 papers, we set up a framework to study the existence of uniformly bounded extension and trace operators for W 1 , p W^{1,p} -functions on randomly perforated domains, where the geometry is assumed to be stationary ergodic. We drop the classical assumption of minimaly smoothness and study stationary geometries which have no global John regularity. For such geometries, uniform extension operators can be defined only from W 1 , p W^{1,p} to W 1 , r W^{1,r} with the strict inequality r < p r<p . In particular, we estimate the L r L^{r} -norm of the extended gradient in terms of the L p L^{p} -norm of the original gradient. Similar relations hold for the symmetric gradients (for ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} -valued functions) and for traces on the boundary. As a byproduct we obtain some Poincaré and Korn inequalities of the same spirit. Such extension and trace operators are important for compactness in stochastic homogenization. In contrast to former approaches and results, we use very weak assumptions: local ( δ , M ) (\delta,M) -regularity to quantify statistically the local Lipschitz regularity and isotropic cone mixing to quantify the density of the geometry and the mesoscopic properties. These two properties are sufficient to reduce the problem of extension operators to the connectivity of the geometry. In contrast to former approaches we do not require a minimal distance between the inclusions and we allow for globally unbounded Lipschitz constants and percolating holes. We will illustrate our method by applying it to the Boolean model based on a Poisson point process and to a Delaunay pipe process, for which we can explicitly estimate the connectivity terms.

.

I thank B. Jahnel for his helpful suggestions on literature. Furthermore,
I thank D.R.M. Renger and G. Friesecke for critical questions that
pushed my research further. Finally I thank the DFG for funding my
reasearch via CRC 1114 Project C05.

1
Introduction

In 1979 Papanicolaou and Varadhan
[
22
]

and Kozlov
[
15
]
for the first time independently
introduced concepts for the averaging of random elliptic operators.
At that time, the periodic homogenization theory had already advanced
to some extend (as can be seen in the book
[
23
]

that had appeared one year before) dealing also with non-uniformly
elliptic operators
[
17
]
and domains
with periodic holes
[
3
]
. The most
recent and most complete work for extension operators on periodically
perforated domains is
[
11
]
.

In contrast, the homogenization on randomly perforated domains is
still open to a large extend. Recent results focus on minimally smooth
domains
[
9
,
24
]

or on decreasing size of the perforations when the smallness parameter
tends to zero
[
8
]
(and references therein).
The main issue in homogenization on perforated domains compared to
classical homogenization problems is compactness. For elasticity,
this is completely open.

The results presented below are meant for application in quenched
convergence. The estimates for the extension and trace operators which
are derived strongly depends on the realization of the geometry -
thus on
\omega
. Nevertheless, if the geometry is stationary, a
corresponding estimate can be achieved for almost every
\omega
.

The Problem

In order to illustrate the issues in stochastic homogenization on
perforated domains, we introduce the following example.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a stationary random open set and let

\varepsilon>0
be the smallness parameter and let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}{\left(\omega\right)}

be an infinitely connected component (i.e. an unbounded connected
domain) of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}{\left(\omega\right)}
. For a bounded open domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
, we
consider
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon}(\omega):=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(\omega)

and
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(\omega):=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\partial\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(\omega)

with outer normal
\nu_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(\omega)}
. For a sufficiently
regular and
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function
u
we denote
\nabla^{s}u:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{\top}\right)

the symmetric part of
\nabla u
. A typical homogenization problem
then is the following::

\displaystyle-\mathrm{div}\left(\left|\nabla^{s}u^{\varepsilon}\right|^{p-2}\nabla^{s}u^{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=g(u^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon}(\omega)\,,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\left(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)\,,

(1.1)

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{s}u^{\varepsilon}\right|^{p-2}\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(\omega)}

\displaystyle=f(u^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(\omega)\,.

Note that for simplicity of illustration, the only randomness that
we consider in this problem is due to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)
.

One way to prove homogenization of (
1.1
)
is to prove
\Gamma
-convergence of

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,\omega}(u)=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon}(\omega)}\left(\frac{1}{p}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}-G(u)\right)+\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}(\omega)}F(u)\,,

in a suitably chosen space where
G^{\prime}=g
and
F^{\prime}=f
. Conceptually,
this implies convergence of the minimizers
u^{\varepsilon}
to a minimizer of
a limit functional but if
G
or
F
are non-monotone, we need compactness.
However, the minimizers are elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon}):=W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{d})

and since this space changes with
\varepsilon
, there is apriori no compactness
of
u^{\varepsilon}
, even though we have uniform apriori estimates on the gradients.

The
canonical
path to circumvent this issue in
periodic

homogenization is via uniformly bounded extension operators
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}:\,W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon})\to W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

that share the property that for some
C>0
independent from
\varepsilon

it holds for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon})
with
u|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0

\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\leq C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon})}\,,\qquad\left\|\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon})}\,,

(1.2)

see
[
11
,
12
]
, combined with uniformly
bounded trace operators, see
[
7
,
9
]
.
Such operators have also been provided for elasticity problems
[
11
,
21
,
30
,
31
]
,
i.e.

\left\|\nabla^{s}\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\leq C\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}}^{\varepsilon})}\,.

The last estimate then allows to use Korn’s inequality combined with
Sobolev’s embedding theorem to find
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0}

weakly in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})
.

What is the classical strategy?

The existing results on extension and trace operators for random domains
are focused on a.s. minimally smooth domains. A connected domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

is minimally smooth
[
26
]
if there exist
(\delta,M)

such that for every
x\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
the set
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(x\right)}

is the graph of a Lipschitz continuous function with Lipschitz constant
less than
M
. It is further assumed that the complement
\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

consists of uniformly bounded sets. This concept leads to almost sure
construction of uniformly bounded extension operators
W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

[
9
]
in the sense that for every bounded

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
and every
u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})
with
u|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0

holds

\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\leq C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\,,\qquad\left\|\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\,,

(1.3)

with
C
independent from
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
. Similarly, one obtains for the
trace
\mathcal{T}
that
[
24
]

\left\|\mathcal{T}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\leq C\left(\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\right)\,.

Using a scaling argument to obtain e.g. (
1.2
),
such extension and trace operators are typically used in order to
treat nonlinearities in homogenization problems.

Why does this work?

The theory cited above is directly connected to the theory of Jones

[
13
]
and Duran and Muschietti
[
5
]

on so-called John domains. These are precisely the bounded domains

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
that admit extension operators
W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

satisfying

\left\|\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\,.

Definition

(John domains)
.

A bounded domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a John domain (a.k.a
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-domain)
if there exists
\varepsilon,\delta>0
such that for every
x,y\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

with
\left|x-y\right|<\delta
there exists a rectifiable path
\gamma:\,[0,1]\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

from
x
to
y
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{length}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|x-y\right|\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\forall t\in(0,1):\quad\inf_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|\gamma(t)-z\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon\left|x-\gamma(t)\right|\left|\gamma(t)-y\right|}{\left|x-y\right|}\,.

Because of the locality implied by
\delta
, it is possible to glue
together local extension operators on John domains such as done in

[
11
]
for periodic or
[
9
]

for minimally smooth domains. In the stochastic case one benefits
a lot from the uniform boundedness of the components of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
,
which allows to split the extension problem into independent extension
problems on uniformly John-regular domains.

Why this is not enough for general random domains!

As one could guess from the emphasis that is put on the above explanations,
random geometries are merely minimally smooth. On an unbounded random
domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, the constant
M
can locally become very large in
points
x\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, while simultaneously,
\delta
can become
very small in the very same
x
. In fact, they are not even “uniformly
John” as the following, yet deterministic example illustrates.

Example 1.1
.

Considering

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}:=\left\{\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\;\exists n\in\mathbb{N}:\,x_{1}-(2n+1)\in(-1,1],\,x_{2}<\max\left\{1,\,n\left|x_{1}-(2n+1)\right|\right\}\right\}

the Lipschitz constant on
(2n,2n+2)
is
n
and it is easy to figure
out that this non-uniformly Lipschitz domain violates the John condition
due to the cups. Hence, a uniform estimate of the form (
1.3
)
cannot exist.

Therefor, an alternative concept to measure the large scale regularity
of a random geometry is needed. Since the classical results do not
excluded the existence of an estimate

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,\qquad\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\mathcal{U}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,

(1.4)

or

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}\mathcal{U}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,\qquad\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\mathcal{U}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,

(1.5)

where
1\leq r<p
and
C
is independent from
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
, such inequalities
will be our goal.

Our results in a nutshell

We will provide inequalities of the form (
1.4
)–(
1.5
)
for a Voronoi-pipe model and for a Boolean model. On the way, we will
provide several concepts and intermediate results that can be reused
in further examples and general considerations such as planed in part
III of this series. Scaled versions (replacing
\varepsilon=m^{-1}
in Theorems

1.16
and
1.18
) of (
1.4
)–(
1.5
)
can be formulated for functions

u\in W_{0,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}^{1,p}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}):=\left\{u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}):\,u|_{(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0\right\}\,,

and will be of the form

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,\qquad\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,

resp.

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla^{s}\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,\qquad\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,

where the support of
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}u
lies within
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon^{\beta}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}

for
\varepsilon
small enough and some arbitrarily chosen but fixed
\beta\in(0,1)
.

Quantifying properties of random geometries

As a replacement for periodicity, we introduce the concept of mesoscopic
regularity of a stationary random open set:

Definition 1.2

(Mesoscopic regularity)
.

Let

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a stationary ergodic random open set, let
\tilde{f}
be
a positive, monotonically decreasing function
\tilde{f}
with
\tilde{f}(R)\to 0

as
R\to\infty
and let
\mathfrak{r}>0
s.t.

\mathbb{P}{\left(\exists x\in\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}:\,\mathbb{B}_{4\sqrt{d}\mathfrak{r}}{\left(x\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}\geq 1-\tilde{f}{\left(R\right)}\,.

(1.6)

Then
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is called
(\mathfrak{r},\tilde{f})
-mesoscopic regular.
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

is called polynomially (exponentially) regular if
1/\tilde{f}
grows
polynomially (exponentially).

As a consequence of Lemmas
3.14
,
3.16

and
3.17
we obtain the following.

Corollary 1.3

(All stationary ergodic random open sets are mesoscopic regular)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)
be a stationary ergodic
random open set. Then there exists
\mathfrak{r}>0
and a monotonically decreasing
function with
\tilde{f}(R)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

is
(\mathfrak{r},\tilde{f})
-mesoscopic regular. Furthermore, there exists
a jointly stationary random point process
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}(\omega)=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}

and for every
a\in\mathbb{N}
it holds
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{2}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

and for all
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
,
a\neq b
, it holds
\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|>2\mathfrak{r}
.
Construct from
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}
a Voronoi tessellation of cells
G_{a}

with diameter
d_{a}=d(x_{a})
. Then for some constant
C>0
and
some monotone decreasing
f:\,(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
and
C>0
with
f(R)\leq C\tilde{f}(C^{-1}R)

it holds

\mathbb{P}{\left(d(x_{a})>D\right)}<f{\left(D\right)}\,.

\mathfrak{r}
,
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}
and
f
from Corollary
1.3

will play a central role in the analysis. We summarize some of these
properties in the following.

Assumption 1.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a Lipschitz domain and assume
there exists
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
be a set of points
having mutual distance
\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|>2\mathfrak{r}
if
a\neq b

and with
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{2}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
for every
a\in\mathbb{N}

(e.g.
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})
, see (
2.51
)).

The second important concept to quantify in a stochastic manner is
that of local Lipschitz regularity.

Definition 1.5

(Local
\left(\delta,M\right)
-Regularity)
.

Let

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set.
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is called
(\delta,M)
-regular

in
p_{0}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
if there exists an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}

and a Lipschitz continuous function
\phi:\,U\to\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz
constant greater or equal to
M
such that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(p_{0}\right)}

is subset of the graph of the function
\varphi:\,U\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,\;\tilde{x}\mapsto\left(\tilde{x},\phi{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\right)

in some suitable coordinate system.

Every Lipschitz domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is
locally
(\delta,M)
-regular

in every
p_{0}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
. In what follows, we bound
\delta

from above by
\mathfrak{r}
only for practical reasons in the proofs. The
following quantities can be derived from local
(\delta,M)
-regularity.

Definition 1.6
.

For a Lipschitz domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for every
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

and
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}

\displaystyle\Delta{\left(p\right)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\delta<\mathfrak{r}}\left\{\exists M>0:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\text{ is }\left(\delta,M\right)\text{-regular in }p\right\}\,,\quad\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}:=\frac{\Delta{\left(p\right)}}{2}\,,

(1.7)

\displaystyle M_{r}(p)

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\eta>r}\inf\left\{M:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\text{ is }\left(\eta,M\right)\text{-regular in }p\right\}\,,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r<\delta{\left(p\right)}}r\left(4M_{r}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2\right)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\,,

(1.9)

If no confusion occurs, we write
\delta=\delta_{\Delta}
. Furthermore,
for
c\in(0,1]
let
\eta(p)=c\delta_{\Delta}(p)
or
\eta(p)=c\rho_{n}(p)
,

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in C^{0,1}(\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})
and define

\displaystyle\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\eta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\,:\;\tilde{x}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\,\text{s.t. }x\in\mathbb{B}_{r{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\right\}\,,

(1.10)

\displaystyle M_{[r,\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sup\left\{M_{r(\tilde{x})}(\tilde{x})\,:\;\tilde{x}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\,\text{s.t. }x\in\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\eta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}\right\}\,,

(1.11)

where
\inf\emptyset=\sup\emptyset:=0
for notational convenience.
We also write
M_{[\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x):=M_{[\eta,\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x)
and
\eta_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\left(x\right)}:=\eta_{[\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\left(x\right)}
.
Of course, we can also consider
M_{[r],\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}:\,p\mapsto M_{r(p)}(p)

as a function on
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, and we will do this once in Lemma

3.8
.

When it comes to application of the abstract results found below,
it is important to have in mind that
\eta
and
M_{r}
are quantities
on
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, while
\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
M_{[r,\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}

are quantities on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Hence, while trivially

\mathbb{P}(\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\in(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))=\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-d}\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|^{-1}\left|\left\{x\in n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}:\,\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\in(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\right\}\right|

(and similarly for
M_{[r,\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
) for every convex bounded open

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
, we have in mind

\mathbb{P}(\eta\in(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))=\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}{\left(\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}{\left(\left\{x\in(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}:\,\eta\in(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\right\}\right)}\,.

We will prove measurability of
\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
M_{[r,\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}

in Lemma
3.11
and see how the weighted
expectations of
\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
M_{[r,\eta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
can be estimated
by weighted expectations of
M
and
\eta
in Lemma
3.12
.

Traces

The first important result is the boundedness of the trace operator.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Lipschitz
domain,
\frac{1}{8}>\mathfrak{r}>0
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded
open set and let
1\leq r<p_{0}<p
. Then the trace operator
\mathcal{T}

satisfies for every
u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|\mathcal{T}u\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|u\right|^{p}+\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}

where for some constant
C_{0}
depending only on
p_{0}
,
p

and
r
and
d
and for
\tilde{\rho}=2^{-5}\rho_{1}
one has

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=C_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{-\frac{1}{p_{0}-r}}\right)^{\frac{p_{0}-r}{p_{0}}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left(1+\tilde{M}_{[\frac{1}{32}\delta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{\left(\frac{1}{p_{0}}+1+\hat{d}\right)\frac{p}{p-p_{0}}}\right)^{\frac{p-p_{0}}{p_{0}p}}\,,

(1.12)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=C_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1+\tilde{M}_{[\frac{1}{32}\delta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{p-r}}\right)^{\frac{p-r}{p}}\,.

(1.13)

Proof.

This is proved in Section
4.6
.
∎

Local Covering of
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

In view of Corollary
3.7
, for every
n=1
or

n=2
there exist a complete covering of
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
by balls

\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{n}\left(p_{i}^{n}\right)}{\left(p_{i}^{n}\right)}
,
\left(p_{i}^{n}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
where
\tilde{\rho}_{n}{\left(p\right)}:=2^{-5}\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}
. We write
\tilde{\rho}_{n,i}:=\tilde{\rho}_{n}(p_{i}^{n})
.

Definition 1.8

(Microscopic regularity and extension order)
.

The inner microscopic regularity
\alpha

is

\alpha:=\inf\left\{\tilde{\alpha}\geq 0:\;\forall p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\exists y\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}:\,\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}(p)/32(1+M_{\tilde{\rho}(p)}(p)^{\tilde{\alpha}})}{\left(p\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}(p)/8}{\left(p\right)}\right\}\,.

In Lemma
3.1
we will see that indeed
\alpha\leq 1
.

Definition 1.9

(Extension order)
.

The geometry is
of extension order

n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
if there exists
C>0
such that for almost every

p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
there exists a local extension operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(p)}{\left(p\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})

\displaystyle\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\rho_{n}(p)}{\left(p\right)})\,,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\rho_{n}(p)}{\left(p\right)})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+M_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(p)}(p)\right)\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(p)}{\left(p\right)})}\,.

(1.14)

The geometry is
of symmetric extension order

n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}

if there exists
C>0
such that for almost every
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

there exists a local extension operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(p)}{\left(p\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\rho_{n}(p)}{\left(p\right)})\,,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{s}\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\rho_{n}(p)}{\left(p\right)})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+M_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(p)}(p)\right)^{2}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(p)}{\left(p\right)})}\,.

(1.15)

Corollary
3.6
shows that every locally
Lipschitz geometry is of extension order
n=1
and every locally
Lipschitz geometry is of symmetric extension order
n=2
. However,
better results for
n
are possible, as we will see below.

Global Tessellation of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

Let
\mathbb{X}=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
be a jointly stationary point
process with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
. In this
work, we will often assume that
\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|>2\mathfrak{r}
for
all
a\neq b
for simplicity in Sections
5

and
6
. The existence of such a process
is always guarantied by Lemmas
3.14

and
3.16
. Its choice in a concrete example is,
however, delicate. Worth mentioning, for most of the theory developed
until the end of Section
4
(Except
for Lemmas
3.17
and
3.18

which are not used before Section
5
),
is completely independent from this mutual minimal distance assumption.

From
\mathbb{X}
we construct a Voronoi tessellation with cells
\left(G_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}

and we chose for each
x_{a}
a radius
\mathfrak{r}_{a}\leq\mathfrak{r}
with
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\subset G_{a}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
.
Again, using Corollary
1.3
, we assume that
\mathfrak{r}_{a}=\mathfrak{r}

is constant for simplicity.

Extensions I: Gradients

Notation 1.10
.

Given
n\in\{0,1\}
and
\alpha\in[0,1]

we chose

\mathfrak{r}_{n,\alpha,i}:=\tilde{\rho}_{n,i}/32(1+M_{\tilde{\rho}_{n,i}}(p_{n,i})^{\alpha})

(1.16)

and some
y_{n,\alpha,i}
such that

B_{n,\alpha,i}:=\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{n,\alpha,i}}{\left(y_{n,\alpha,i}\right)}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\tilde{\rho}_{n,i}}{\left(p_{n,i}\right)}\,.

(1.17)

and for every
i
and
a
, we define

\tau_{n,\alpha,i}u:=\fint_{B_{n,\alpha,i}}u\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}_{a}u:=\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{a}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}u\,,

local averages close to
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and in
x_{a}
. We say that

x_{a}\sim\sim x_{b}
if
G_{a}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{b}\right)}\neq\emptyset

and we say
x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})
if
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset
.
Based on (
4.14
) we obtain the following extension
result.

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a stationary
ergodic random Lipschitz domain such that Assumption
1.4

holds for
\mathbb{X}=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
has microscopic
regularity
\alpha
with extension order
n
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

be a bounded open set with
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}{\left(0\right)}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
and let

1\leq r<p
. Furthermore, let

\mathbb{E}{\left(\left(\left(1+M_{[\frac{3\delta}{8},\frac{\delta}{8}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{nd}\left(1+M_{[\frac{1}{8}\delta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{r}\left(1+M_{[\tilde{\rho}_{n}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{\alpha(d-1)}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-r}}\right)}<\infty

then there exist
C>0
depending only on
d
,
r
and
p
such
that for a.e.
\omega
there exists an extension operator
\mathcal{U}_{\omega}:\,W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))\to W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and
C_{\omega}>0
such that for every
m\geq 1
and every
u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))

with
u|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)\backslash m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0
it holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla\left(\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right)\right|^{r}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}

\displaystyle+C\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{a}\tilde{\rho}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}^{-r}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{2}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{n,i}}{\left(p_{n,i}\right)}}\left|\tau_{n,\alpha,i}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}u\right|^{r}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left|\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\sum_{a}\sum_{a\sim\sim b}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}u\right|\right|^{r}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right|^{r}\leq

\displaystyle C_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Lemma
4.7
.
∎

In case one is interested in a weaker estimate on the extension operator,
we propose the following:

Theorem 1.12
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.11
let additionally

\mathbb{E}{\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}^{-\frac{rp}{p-r}}\right)}<\infty

then there exists an extension operator
\mathcal{U}_{\omega}:\,W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))\to W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that for every
m\geq 1
and every
u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))

with
u|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)\backslash m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0
it holds

\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left(\left|\nabla\left(\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right)\right|^{r}+\left|\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right|^{r}\right)\leq C_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}}\left(\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}+\left|u\right|^{p}\right)\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the proof of Lemma
4.7
,
replacing
M_{a}u
in the definition of
\mathcal{U}u
by
0
.
∎

Percolation and Connectivity

The terms depending on
\left|\tau_{n,\alpha,i}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}u\right|

or
\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}u\right|
appearing on the right hand side
in Theorem
1.11
need to be replaced by an integral over

\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}
. Here, the pathwise topology of the geometry
comes into play. By this we mean that we have to integrate the gradient
of
u
over a path connecting e.g.
p_{i}
and
x_{a}
. Here, the
mesoscopic properties of the geometry will play a role. In particular,
we need pathwise connectedness of the random domain, a phenomenon
which is known as percolation in the theory of random sets. We will
discuss two different examples to see that these terms can indeed
be handled in application, but shift a general discussion of arbitrary
geometries to a later publication.

Extensions II: Symmetric gradients

We now turn to the situation that
u
is a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function
and that the given PDE system yields only estimates for
\nabla^{s}u=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{T}\right)
.
We introduce the following quantities:

Definition 1.13
.

Given
n\in\{0,1,2\}
and
\alpha\in[0,1]

such that such that (
1.17
) holds for
\mathfrak{r}_{i}=\mathfrak{r}_{n,\alpha,i}

for every
i
let for
i,a

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}^{\bot}_{n,\alpha,i}u

\displaystyle:=\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{n,\alpha,i}}{\left(y_{n,\alpha,i}\right)}}\left(\nabla u-\nabla^{s}u\right)\,,

\displaystyle\left[\tau_{n,\alpha,i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right](x):=

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}^{\bot}_{n,\alpha,i}u\,\left(x-y_{2,i}\right)+\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{n,\alpha,i}}{\left(y_{n,\alpha,i}\right)}}u\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}^{\bot}_{a}u

\displaystyle:=\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{a}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left(\nabla u-\nabla^{s}u\right)\,,

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right](x):=

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}^{\bot}_{a}u\,\left(x-x_{a}\right)+\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{a}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}u\,.

Using above introduced notation and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}
do denote
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued
Sobolev spaces, we find the following.

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a stationary
ergodic random Lipschitz domain such that Assumption
1.4

holds for
\mathbb{X}=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
has microscopic
regularity
\alpha
with symmetric extension order
n\leq 2
. Let

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set with
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}{\left(0\right)}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}

and let
1\leq r<p_{0}<p
. Furthermore, let

\mathbb{E}{\left(\left(\left(1+M_{[\frac{3\delta}{8},\frac{\delta}{8}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{nd}\left(1+M_{[\frac{1}{8}\delta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{2r}\left(1+M_{[\tilde{\rho}_{n}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{\alpha(d-1)}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-r}}\right)}<\infty

then hen there exist
C>0
depending only on
d
,
r
,
s
and

p
such that for a.e.
\omega
there exists an extension operator

\mathcal{U}_{\omega}:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and
C_{\omega}>0
such that for every
m\geq 1
and every
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))

with
u|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0
it holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right)\right|^{r}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\sum_{a}\sum_{i\neq 0}\rho_{1,i}^{-r}\chi_{A_{1,i}}\chi_{\mathfrak{A}_{1,a}}\left|\tau_{n,\alpha,i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{r}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\sum_{l=1}^{d}\sum_{a:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{a}>0}\sum_{b:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{b}<0}\frac{\partial_{l}\Phi_{a}\left|\partial_{l}\Phi_{b}\right|}{D_{l+}^{\Phi}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right|^{r}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right|^{r}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,

Proof.

This is a consequence of Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Theorem 1.15
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.14
let additionally

\mathbb{E}{\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}^{-\frac{rp}{p-r}}\right)}<\infty

then there exists an extension operator
\mathcal{U}_{\omega}:\,W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))\to W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that for every
m\geq 1
and every
u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega))

with
u|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)\backslash m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\equiv 0
it holds

\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left(\left|\nabla\left(\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right)\right|^{r}+\left|\mathcal{U}_{\omega}u\right|^{r}\right)\leq C_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}}\left(\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}+\left|u\right|^{p}\right)\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the proof of Lemma
4.7
,
replacing
M_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u
in the definition of
\mathcal{U}u
by
0
.
∎

Discussion: Random Geometries and Applicability of the Method

In Section
6
we discuss two standard models
from the theory of stochastic geometries. The first one is a system
of random pipes: Starting from a Poisson point process and deleting
all points with nearest neighbor closer than
2\mathfrak{r}
and introducing
the Delaunay neighboring condition on the points, every two neighbors
are connect through a pipe of random thickness
2\delta,
where
\delta

is distributed i.i.d among the pipes and we complete the geometry
by adding a ball of radius
\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{2}
around each point. Defining
for bounded open domains
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

u\in W_{0,\partial(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}):=\left\{u\in W^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}):\,u|_{\partial(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\equiv 0\right\}\,,

and using
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}
instead of
W
for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions, we find
our first result:

Theorem 1.16
.

In the pipe model of Section
6.1

let
\mathbb{P}(\delta(x,y)<\delta_{0})\leq C_{\delta}\delta_{0}^{\beta}

and let
1\leq r<s<p
be such that
\max\left\{\frac{p\left(s+d\right)}{p-s},\frac{p(2d-s-1)}{p-s}\right\}\leq\beta

and
\frac{sr}{s-r}\leq\beta+d-1
. Then
\alpha=n=0
both for extension
and symmetric extension order and there almost surely exists an extension
operator
\mathcal{U}:\,W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\to W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and constants

C,R>1
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and every
u\in W_{0,\partial(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

it holds

\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\left(\mathcal{U}u\right)\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

Furthermore there almost surely exists an extension operator
\mathcal{U}:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and a constant
C>0
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and every
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{0,\partial(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla^{s}\left(\mathcal{U}u\right)\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

In both cases for every
\beta\in(0,1)
the following holds: for
some
m_{0}>1
depending on
\omega
and every
m>m_{0}
the support
of
\mathcal{U}u
lies within
\mathbb{B}_{m^{1-\beta}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}
.

Proof.

The proof is given at the very end of Section
6.1
.
∎

Corollary 1.17
.

If
\mathbb{P}(\delta(x,y)<\delta_{0})\leq C_{\delta}e^{-\gamma\delta_{0}^{-1}}

then the last theorem holds for every
1\leq r<p
.

In Section
6.2
we study the Boolean model
based on a Poisson point process in the percolation case. Introduced
in Example
2.48
we will consider a Poisson
point process
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}(\omega)=\left(x_{i}(\omega)\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

with intensity
\lambda
(recall Example
2.48
).
To each point
x_{i}
a random ball
B_{i}=\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(x_{i}\right)}
is assigned
and the family
\mathbb{B}:=\left(B_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is called the Poisson
ball process. We say that
x_{i}\sim x_{j}
if
\left|x_{i}-x_{j}\right|<2
.
In case
\lambda>\lambda_{c}
the union of these balls has a unique
infinite connected component (that means we have percolation) and
we denote
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}
the sellection of all points that contribute
to the infinite component and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}\left(\omega\right):=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}}B_{i}

this infinite open set and seek for a corresponding uniform extension
operator. The connectedness of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}
is hereby essential.
We use results from percolation theory that otherwise would not hold.

Here we can show that the micro- and mesoscopic assumptions are fulfilled,
at least in case
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is given as the union of balls. If we choose

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
as the complement of the balls, the situation becomes more
involved. On one hand, Theorem
6.8

shows that
\alpha
and
n
change in an unfortunate way. Furthermore,
the connectivity estimate remains open. However, some of these problems
might be overcome using a Matern modification of the Poisson process.
For the moment, we state the following.

Theorem 1.18
.

In the boolean model of Section
6.2

it holds
\alpha=0
in case
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}
and both the extension
order and the symmetric extension order are
n=0
. If
d<p
and

\frac{pr}{p-r}<2,\quad r<d+2

Then there almost surely exists an extension operator
\mathcal{U}:\,W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\to W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and a constant
C>0
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and every
u\in W_{0,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla\left(\mathcal{U}u\right)\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

If furthermore

r<\frac{d+2}{2}

then there almost surele exists an extension operator
\mathcal{U}:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and a constant
C>0
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and every
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{0,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\frac{1}{\left|m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}\left(\mathcal{U}u\right)\right|^{r}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m^{d}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

In both cases for every
\beta\in(0,1)
the following holds: for
some
m_{0}>1
depending on
\omega
and every
m>m_{0}
the support
of
\mathcal{U}u
lies within
\mathbb{B}_{m^{1-\beta}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}
.

Proof.

The proof is given at the very end of Section
6.2
.
∎

Notes

Structure of the article

We close the introduction by providing an overview over the article
and its main contributions. In Section
2
we
collect some basic concepts and inequalities from the theory of Sobolev
spaces, random geometries and discrete and continuous ergodic theory.
We furthermore establish local regularity properties for what we call

\eta
-regular sets, as well as a related covering theorem in Section

2.8
. In Section
2.13

we will demonstrate that stationary ergodic random open sets induce
stationary processes on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, a fact which is used later in the
construction of the mesoscopic Voronoi tessellation in Section
3.2
.

In Section
3
we introduce the regularity
concepts of this work. More precisely, in Section
3.1

we introduce the concept of local
\left(\delta,M\right)
-regularity
and use the theory of Section
2.8
in order
to establish a local covering result for
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, which will
allow us to infer most of our extension and trace results. In Section

3.2
we show how isotropic cone mixing
geometries allow us to construct a stationary Voronoi tessellation
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that all related quantities like “diameter” of
the cells are stationary variables whose expectation can be expressed
in terms of the isotropic cone mixing function
f
. Moreover we prove
the important integration Lemma
3.18
.

In Sections
4
–
5

we finally provide the aforementioned extension operators and prove
estimates for these extension operators and for the trace operator.
In Section
6
we study the sample geometries.

A Remark on Notation

This article uses concepts from partial differential equations, measure
theory, probability theory and random geometry. Additionally, we introduce
concepts which we believe have not been introduced before. This makes
it difficult to introduce readable self contained notation (the most
important aspect being symbols used with different meaning) and enforces
the use of various different mathematical fonts. Therefore, we provide
an index of notation at the end of this work. As a rough orientation,
the reader may keep the following in mind:

We use the standard notation
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{Z}
for natural
(
>0
), rational, real and integer numbers.
\mathbb{P}
denotes a probability
measure,
\mathbb{E}
the expectation. Furthermore, we use special notation
for some geometrical objects, i.e.
\mathbb{T}^{d}=[0,1)^{d}
for the torus
(
\mathbb{T}
equipped with the topology of the torus),
\mathbb{I}^{d}=(0,1)^{d}

the open interval as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(we often omit the index
d
),

\mathbb{B}
a ball,
\mathbb{C}
a cone and
\mathbb{X}
a set of points. In the context
of finite sets
A
, we write
\#A
for the number of elements.

Bold large symbols (
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
,
\dots
) refer to open
subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or to closed subsets with
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}=\partial\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}
.
The Greek letter
\Gamma
refers to a
d-1
dimensional manifold
(aside from the notion of
\Gamma
-convergence).

Calligraphic symbols (
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{U}
,
\dots
) usually refer
to operators and large Gothic symbols (
\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C},\dots
) indicate
topological spaces, except for
\mathfrak{A}
.

Outlook

This work is the first part of a triology. In part II, we will see
how to apply the extension and trace operators introduced above.

In part III we will discuss general quantifyable properties of the
geometry that are eventually accessible also to computer algorithms
that will allow to predict homogenization behavior of random geometries.

2
Preliminaries

We first collect some notation and mathematical concepts which will
be frequently used throughout this paper. We first start with the
standard geometric objects, which will be labeled by bold letters.

2.1
Fundamental Notation and Geometric Objects

Throughout this work, we use
\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{i}\right)_{i=1,\dots d}

for the Euclidean basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By
C>0
we denote any constant
that depends on
p
and
d
but no further dependencies unless explicitly
mentioned. Such mentioning may expressed in some cases through the
notation
C(a,b,\dots)
. Furthermore, we use the following notation.

Unit cube
The torus
\mathbb{T}=[0,1)^{d}
is quipped with
the topology of the metric
d(x,y)=\min_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left|x-y+z\right|
.
In contrast, the open interval
\mathbb{I}^{d}:=(0,1)^{d}
is considered
as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We often omit the index
d
if this does not
provoke confusion.

Balls
Given a metric space
\left(M,d\right)
we denote

\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}
the
open ball around
x\in M
with radius
r>0
. The surface of the
unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Furthermore, we denote for every

A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(A\right)}:=\bigcup_{x\in A}\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}
.

Points
A sequence of points will be labeled by
\mathbb{X}:=\left(x_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

A cone
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is usually labeled by
\mathbb{C}
. In
particular, we define for a vector
\nu
of unit length,
0<\alpha<\frac{\pi}{2}

and
R>0
the cone

\mathbb{C}_{\nu,\alpha,R}(x):=\left\{z\in\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(x\right)}\,:\;z\cdot\nu>\left|z\right|\cos\alpha\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{C}_{\nu,\alpha}(x):=\mathbb{C}_{\nu,\alpha,\infty}(x)\,.

Inner and outer hull
We use balls of radius
r>0

to define for a closed set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the sets

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{r}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\mathrm{dist}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)\leq r\right\}\,,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{-r}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\left[\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}\right]:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\mathrm{dist}\left(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)\geq r\right\}\,.

One can consider these sets as inner and outer hulls of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
. The
last definition resembles a concept of “negative distance” of

x\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
to
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and “positive distance” of
x\not\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

to
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
. For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
we denote
\mathrm{conv}(A)
the
closed convex hull of
A
.

The natural geometric measures we use in this work are the Lebesgue
measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, written
\left|A\right|
for
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure, denoted by
\mathcal{H}^{k}
on
k
-dimensional
submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(for
k\leq d
).

2.2
Simple Local Extensions and Traces

In the following, we formulate some extension and trace results. Although
it is well known how such results are proved and the proofs are standard,
we include them for completeness since we are particularly interested
in the dependence of the operator norm on the local Lipschitz regularity
of the boundary.

The following is well known:

Lemma 2.1
.

For every
1\leq p\leq\infty
there
exists
C_{p}>0
such that for every
R>0
there exists an extension
operator
\mathcal{U}:\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{2R}{\left(0\right)})
such that

\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{2R}{\left(0\right)})}\leq C_{p}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})}\,.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set and let
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and

\delta>0
be a constant such that
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(p\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

is graph of a Lipschitz function. We denote

\displaystyle M(p,\delta)

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{M:\,\,\exists\phi:U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ }\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{:=\inf}\left.\phi\,\text{Lipschitz, with constant }M\text{ s.t. }\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(p\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\text{ is graph of }\phi\right\}\,.

(2.2)

Remark 2.2
.

For every
p
, the function
M(p,\cdot)
is monotone increasing
in
\delta
.

Lemma 2.3

(Uniform Extension for Balls)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open
set,
0\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and assume there exists
\delta>0
,
M>0

and an open domain
U\subset\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that

\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
is graph of a Lipschitz function

\varphi:\,U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form
\varphi(\tilde{x})=(\tilde{x},\phi(\tilde{x}))

in
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
with Lipschitz constant
M
and
\varphi(0)=0
.
Writing
x=(\tilde{x},x_{d})
and defining
\rho=\delta\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{-1}

there exist an extension operator

\left(\mathcal{U}u\right)(x)=\begin{cases}u(x)&\mbox{ if }x_{d}<\phi(\tilde{x})\\
u\left(\tilde{x},{-}x_{d}+2\phi(\tilde{x})\right)&\mbox{ if }x_{d}>\phi(\tilde{x})\end{cases}\,,

(2.3)

such that for

\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right):=\left\{\left(\tilde{x},{-}x_{d}+2\phi(\tilde{x})\right)\,:\;\left(\tilde{x},x_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right\}\subset\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\,,

(2.4)

and for every
p\in[1,\infty]
the operator

\mathcal{U}:\,W^{1,p}{\left(\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\right)}\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)})\,,

is continuous with

\left\|\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\leq\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\right)}\,,\qquad\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\leq 2M\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\right)}\,.

(2.5)

Remark 2.4
.

In case
\phi(\tilde{x})\geq 0
we find
\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\subset\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

In case
\phi(\tilde{x})\equiv 0
we consider the extension operator

\mathcal{U}_{+}:\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times({-}\infty,0))\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

having the form (compare also
[
6
, chapter 5]
,
[
1
]
)

\left(\mathcal{U}_{+}u\right)(x)=\begin{cases}u(x)&\mbox{ if }x_{d}<0\\
u\left(\tilde{x},{-}x_{d}\right)&\mbox{ if }x_{d}>0\end{cases}\,.

The general case follows from transformation.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set,
0\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

and assume there exists
\delta>0
,
M>0
and an open domain
U\subset\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}

such that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
is graph of a Lipschitz
function
\varphi:\,U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form
\varphi(\tilde{x})=(\tilde{x},\phi(\tilde{x}))

in
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
with Lipschitz constant
M
and
\varphi(0)=0

and define
\rho=\delta\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{-1}
. Writing
x=(\tilde{x},x_{d})

we consider the trace operator
\mathcal{T}:\,C^{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\right)\to C\left(\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}\right)
.
For every
p\in[1,\infty]
and every
r<\frac{p\left(1-d\right)}{\left(p-d\right)}

the operator
\mathcal{T}
can be continuously extended to

\mathcal{T}:\,W^{1,p}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\right)\to L^{r}(\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)})\,,

such that

\left\|\mathcal{T}u\right\|_{L^{r}(\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)})}\leq C_{r,p}\rho^{\frac{d\left(p-r\right)}{rp}-\frac{1}{r}}\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{\frac{1}{r}+1}\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,p}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\right)}\,.

(2.6)

Proof.

We proceed similar to the proof of Lemma
2.3
.

Step 1: Writing
B_{r}=\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}
together with
B_{r}^{-}=\left\{x\in B_{r}:\,x_{d}<0\right\}

and
\Sigma_{r}:=\left\{x\in B_{r}:\,x_{d}=0\right\}
we recall
the standard estimate

\left(\int_{\Sigma_{1}}\left|u\right|^{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\leq C_{r,p}\left(\left(\int_{B_{1}^{-}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{B_{1}^{-}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)\,,

which leads to

\left(\int_{\Sigma_{\rho}}\left|u\right|^{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\leq C_{r,p}\rho^{\frac{d\left(p-r\right)}{rp}-\frac{1}{r}}\left(\rho\left(\int_{B_{\rho}^{-}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{B_{\rho}^{-}}\left|u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)\,.

Step 2: Using the transformation rule and the fact that
1\leq\left|\det D\varphi\right|\leq\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}

we infer (
2.6
) similar to Step 2 in the proof
of Lemma
2.3
.

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}}\left|u\right|^{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{\frac{1}{r}}\left(\int_{\Sigma_{\rho}}\left|u\circ\varphi\right|^{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r,p}\rho^{\frac{d\left(p-r\right)}{rp}-\frac{1}{r}}\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{\frac{1}{r}}\left(\rho\left(\int_{B_{\rho}^{-}}\left|\nabla\left(u\circ\varphi\right)\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{B_{\rho}^{-}}\left|u\circ\varphi\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{r,p}\rho^{\frac{d\left(p-r\right)}{rp}-\frac{1}{r}}\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{\frac{1}{r}+1}\,\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\left(\rho\left(\int_{B_{\rho}^{-}}\left|\left(\nabla u\right)\circ\varphi\right|^{p}\det D\varphi\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{B_{\rho}^{-}}\left|u\circ\varphi\right|^{p}\det D\varphi\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

and from this we conclude the Lemma with
\varphi^{-1}(B_{\rho}^{-})\subset\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
.
∎

2.3
Local Nitsche-Extensions

In this work, we will use bold letters for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function
spaces. In particular, we introduce for
1\leq p\leq\infty

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{L}}^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\displaystyle:=L^{p}(u;\mathbb{R}^{d})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\displaystyle:=\left\{u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{L}}^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})\,:\;\nabla u\in L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\right)}\right\}\,.

From
[
5
]
we know that on general Lipschitz domains
an estimate like the following holds:

Lemma 2.6
.

For every
1\leq p\leq\infty

there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the dimension
d\geq 2

such that the following holds: For every radius
R>0
there exists
an extension operator
\mathcal{U}_{R}:\,W^{1,p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}\to W^{1,p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{2R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}

such that

\left\|\nabla^{s}\left(\mathcal{U}_{R}u\right)\right\|_{W^{1,p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{2R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}\leq C\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{W^{1,p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}\,.

Again, we will need a refined estimate on extensions on Lipschitz
domains which explicitly accounts for the local Lipschitz constant.

Lemma 2.7

(Uniform Nitsche-Extension for Balls)
.

For every
d\geq 2

there exists a constant
C_{\mathcal{N}}
depending only on the dimension

d
such that the following holds: Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open
set,
0\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and assume there exists
\delta>0
,
M>0

and an open domain
U\subset\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that

\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
is graph of a Lipschitz function

\varphi:\,U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the form
\varphi(\tilde{x})=(\tilde{x},\phi(\tilde{x}))

in
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
with Lipschitz constant
M
and
\varphi(0)=0
.
Writing
x=(\tilde{x},x_{d})
and defining
\rho=\delta\sqrt{4M^{2}+2}^{-1}

and

\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right):=\left\{\left(\tilde{x},x_{d}\right)\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\,:\;\left|\tilde{x}\right|<\rho,\,x_{d}\leq C_{\mathcal{N}}(1+M^{2})\right\}\,,

(2.7)

and for every
p\in[1,\infty]
there exists a continuous operator

\mathcal{U}:\,W^{1,p}{\left(\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\right)}\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)})\,,

such that for some constant
C
independent from
\left(\delta,M\right)

and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
it holds

\left\|\nabla^{s}\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})}\leq C\left(1+M\right)^{2}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\right)}\,.

(2.8)

Remark 2.8
.

In case
\phi(\tilde{x})\geq 0
the proof
reveals
\mathcal{A}\left(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\rho\right)\subset\mathbb{B}_{c\rho}{\left(0\right)}
for some

c
depending only on the dimension
d
.

In order to prove such a result we need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.9

(
[
26
]
Chapter 6 Section 1 Theorem 2)
.

There exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0

such that for every open set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with local Lipschitz
boundary there exists a function
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}^{\complement}}\to\mathbb{R}

with

\displaystyle c_{1}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}{\left(x,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(x)\,,

\displaystyle\forall i\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\,,

\displaystyle\forall i,k\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}\partial_{k}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\left|d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(x)\right|^{-1}\,.

From the theory presented by Stein
[
26
]
we will
not get an explicit form of
C_{\mathcal{N}}
but only an upper bound that
grows exponentially with dimension
d
.

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

We use an idea by Nitsche
[
20
]
, which we transfer
from
p=2
to the general case, thereby explicitly quantifying the
influence of
M
. For simplicity we write
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}:=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}

and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}:=\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and
assume that
x\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}
iff
x\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}
and
x_{d}<\phi(\tilde{x})
.

As observed by Nitsche, it holds

\forall x\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}:\quad 0<\left(1+M^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(x_{d}-\phi(\tilde{x})\right)\leq\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}})\leq x_{d}-\phi(\tilde{x})\,,

and together with Lemma
2.9
, we can define
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x):=2c_{2}\left(1+M^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}(x)

and find for
c>\max\left\{\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}},4c_{2}c_{3}\right\}

that

\displaystyle 2\left(x_{d}-\phi(\tilde{x})\right)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)

\displaystyle\leq c\left(1+M^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(x_{d}-\phi(\tilde{x})\right)\,,

\displaystyle\forall i\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left(1+M^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\forall i,k\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}\partial_{k}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left(1+M^{2}\right)\left|d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\right|^{-1}\,.

If
\psi\in C([1,2])
satisfies

\int_{1}^{2}\psi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=1\,,\qquad\int_{1}^{2}t\,\psi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

(2.9)

Nitsche introduced
x_{\lambda}:=(\tilde{x},x_{d}-\lambda d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x))

and proposed the following extension on
x\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}
:

u_{i}(x):=\int_{1}^{2}\psi(\lambda)\left(u_{i}(x_{\lambda})+\lambda u_{d}(x_{\lambda})\,\partial_{i}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}\lambda\,.

One can quickly verify that this maps
C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}})

onto
C{\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}}\right)}
. In what follows, we write
\varepsilon[u](x):=\nabla^{s}u(x)

and particularly
\varepsilon_{ij}[u](x):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{i}u_{j}+\partial_{j}u_{i}\right)

as well as
\varepsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}[u](x)=\varepsilon_{ij}[u](x_{\lambda})

for
x\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}
. Then for
x\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{ij}[u](x)=\int_{1}^{2}\psi(\lambda)

\displaystyle\left(\varepsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}(x)+\lambda\partial_{i}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,\varepsilon_{jd}^{\lambda}(x)+\lambda\partial_{j}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,\varepsilon_{id}^{\lambda}(x)\right.

(2.10)

\displaystyle\;\left.+\lambda^{2}\partial_{i}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,\partial_{j}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,\varepsilon_{dd}^{\lambda}(x)+\lambda\partial_{i}\partial_{j}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,u_{d}(x_{\lambda})\right)

(2.11)

From the fundamental theorem of calculus we find

u_{d}(x_{\lambda})=u_{d}(x_{1})+\delta(\tilde{x})\int_{1}^{\lambda}\partial_{d}u_{d}(x_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

which leads by (
2.9
) to

\int_{1}^{2}\psi(\lambda)\lambda\partial_{i}\partial_{j}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,u_{d}(x_{\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\,d_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},M}(x)\int_{1}^{2}\varepsilon_{dd}[u](x_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mu}^{2}\psi(\lambda)\lambda\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\,.

We may now apply
\left|\,\cdot\,\right|^{p}
on both sides of (
2.10
),
integrate over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}
and use
the integral transformation theorem for each
\lambda
to find

\left\|\varepsilon[u]\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}^{\complement}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\rho}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}\leq C\left(1+M^{2}\right)\left\|\varepsilon[u]\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\delta}\right)}}\,.

∎

2.4
Poincaré Inequalities

We denote for bounded open
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

W_{(0),r}^{1,p}(A):=\left\{u\in W^{1,p}(A)\,:\;\exists x:\,B_{r}(x)\subset A\;\vee\;\fint_{B_{r}(x)}u=0\right\}\,.

Note that this is not a linear vector space.

Lemma 2.10
.

For every
p\in[1,\infty)
there exists

C_{p}>0
such that the following holds: Let
0<r<R
and
x\in\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}

such that
\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}
then for every
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(R^{p}\frac{R^{d-1}}{r^{d-1}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}+\frac{R^{d}}{r^{d}}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)})}^{p}\right)\,,

(2.12)

and for every
u\in W_{(0),r}^{1,p}((\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})
it holds

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\leq C_{p}R^{p}\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{1-d}\left(1+\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{p-1}\right)\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\,.

(2.13)

Remark
.

In case
p\geq d
we find that (
2.13
) holds
iff
u(x)=0
for some
x\in\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}
.

Proof.

In a first step, we assume
x=0
and
R=1
. The underlying idea
of the proof is to compare every
u(y)
,
y\in\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}

with
u(rx)
. In particular, we obtain for
y\in\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}

that

u(y)=u(ry)+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u{\left(ry+t(1-r)y\right)}\cdot(1-r)y\,\mathrm{d}t

and hence by Jensen’s inequality

\left|u(y)\right|^{p}\leq C\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left|\nabla u{\left(ry+t(1-r)y\right)}\right|^{p}(1-r)^{p}\left|y\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t+\left|u(ry)\right|^{p}\right)\,.

We integrate the last expression over
\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}

and find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}}\left|u(y)\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{r}^{1}C\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left|\nabla u{\left(rs\nu+t(1-r)s\nu\right)}\right|^{p}(1-r)^{p}s^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)s^{d-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\nu

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}}\left|u(ry)\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{r}^{1}C\left(\int_{rs}^{s}\left|\nabla u{\left(t\nu\right)}\right|^{p}(1-r)^{p-1}s^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)s^{d-1}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}}\left|u(ry)\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{r}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\;s^{d-1}\frac{1}{\left(rs\right)^{d-1}}\int_{rs}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\;t^{d-1}\int_{S^{d-1}}\left|\nabla u{\left(t\nu\right)}\right|^{p}(1-r)^{p-1}s^{p-1}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}}\left|u(ry)\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}+\frac{1}{r^{d}}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\,.

For general
x\in\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}
, use the extension operator
\mathcal{U}:\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})\to W^{1,p}(B_{4}(0))

such that
\left\|\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{W^{1,p}(B_{4}(0))}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})}

and
\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{W^{1,p}(B_{4}(0))}\leq C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})}
.
Since
\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\subset B_{2}(x)\subset B_{4}(0)
we infer

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\leq\left\|\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(B_{2}(x))}^{p}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\left\|\nabla\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(B_{2}(x))}^{p}+\frac{1}{r^{d}}\left\|\mathcal{U}u\right\|_{L^{p}(B_{r}(x))}^{p}\right)\,.

and hence (
2.12
). Furthermore, since there
holds
\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\leq C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}

for every
u\in W_{(0)}^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})
, a scaling argument shows

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}\leq Cr^{p}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)})}^{p}

for every
u\in W_{(0),r}^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)})
and hence (
2.13
).
For general
R>0
use a scaling argument.
∎

A similar argument leads to the following, where we remark that the
difference in the appearing of
\frac{1}{r}
is due to the fact,
that integrating the cylinder needs no surface element
r^{d-1}
.

Corollary 2.11
.

For every
p\in[1,\infty)
and
r>0

there exists
C_{p}>0
such that the following holds: Let
r<L
,

P_{L,r}:=\mathbb{B}_{r}^{d-1}{\left(0\right)}\times(0,L)
and
x\in P_{L,r}
such that

\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\subset P_{L,r}
then for every
u\in W^{1,p}(P_{L,r})

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(L^{p}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}+\frac{L}{r}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)})}^{p}\right)\,,

(2.14)

and if additionally
\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}}u=0
then

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(L^{p}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}+Lr^{p-1}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)})}^{p}\right)\,,

(2.15)

Let
y\in P_{L,r}
such that
\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(y\right)}\subset P_{L,r}
then for
every
u\in W^{1,p}(P_{L,r})

\left|\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(y\right)}}u-\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}}u\right|^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(L^{p-1}r^{1-d}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\right)\,.

(2.16)

2.5
Korn Inequalities

We introduce on open sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the Sobolev space

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\nabla^{\bot}(0)}^{1,p}{\left(A\right)}:=\left\{u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}{\left(A\right)}:\;\forall i,j:\,\int_{A}\partial_{i}u_{j}-\partial_{j}u_{i}=0\right\}\,.

To the authors best knowledge, the following is the most general Korn
inequality in literature.

Theorem 2.12

(
[
5
]
Theorem 2.7 and Corollary 2.8)
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)

and
\tilde{\delta}>0
. Then there exists a constant
C_{p}>0
depending
only on
d
,
p
,
\varepsilon
and
\tilde{\delta}
such that for every
bounded open set
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\delta>0
such that
\delta/\mathrm{diam}A\geq\tilde{\delta}

and with the property

\left.\begin{aligned} &\forall x,y\in A,\,\left|x-y\right|<\delta:\quad\exists\gamma\in C^{1}([0,1];A),\,\gamma(0)=x,\,\gamma(1)=y\text{ such that:}\\
&\qquad l(\gamma)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|x-y\right|\quad\text{and}\quad\forall t\in(0,1):\,\mathrm{dist}{\left(\gamma(t),\partial A\right)}\geq\frac{\varepsilon\left|x-\gamma(t)\right|\left|y-\gamma(t)\right|}{x-y}\end{aligned}\right\}

(2.17)

it holds

\forall u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\nabla^{\bot}(0)}^{1,p}{\left(A\right)}:\,\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A\right)}}\leq C_{p}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A\right)}}\,.

(2.18)

Remark 2.13
.

In the original work the claimed dependence of
C_{p}
was on
d
,

p
,
\varepsilon
,
\delta
and
A
with the observation that (
2.18
)
is invariant under scaling of
A
. However, this scale invariance
results in the dependence on
d
,
p
,
\varepsilon
and
\delta/\mathrm{diam}A

since
\varepsilon
,
p
and
d
are not sensitive to scaling of
A
.

Definition 2.14
.

Domains
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying (
2.17
) for some

\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\delta>0
are called
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-John domains
or simply John domains.

Corollary 2.15
.

For every
1\leq p\leq\infty
there exists
C_{p}
depending only
on
d
and
p
such that for every bounded open convex set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

the estimate (
2.18
) holds.

We furthermore introduce the set

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}(A):=\left\{u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(A)\,:\;\exists x:\,\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\subset A\;\vee\;\forall i,j:\,\int_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}}\partial_{i}u_{j}-\partial_{j}u_{i}=0\right\}

which is
not
a vector space.

Lemma 2.16

(Mixed Korn inequality)
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
.
Then there exists a constant
\tilde{C}_{p}>0
depending only on

d
,
p
,
\varepsilon
and
\delta
such that for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-John
domain
A\subset\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}
and for every
r\in(0,1)
and every
x\in A

with
\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\subset A
it holds

\forall u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}{\left(A\right)}:\,\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A\right)}}\leq\tilde{C}_{p}\left(\frac{\left|A\right|}{r^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A\right)}}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\right)}}\right)\,.

(2.19)

Furthermore,

\forall u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}(A):\,\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A\right)}}\leq\tilde{C}_{p}\left(\frac{\left|A\right|}{\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|r^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A\right)}}\right)\,.

(2.20)

Unfortunately, we do not have a reference for a comparable Lemma in
the literature except for
[
25
]
in case
p=2
.
The author strongly supposes a proof must exist somewhere, however,
we provide it for completeness.

Proof.

Let
C_{p}
be the constant from Theorem
2.12
for
domains with a diameter less than
2
and suppose (
2.19
)
was wrong. Then there exists a sequence of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-John domains

A_{n}\subset\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}
with
x_{n}\in A_{n}
,
r_{n}\in(0,1)
with

\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\subset A_{n}
and functions
u_{n}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}

such that

1=\left\|\nabla u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\geq C_{p}\left(\frac{\left|A_{n}\right|}{\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|r_{n}^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}n\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}+\left\|\nabla u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\right)}}\right)\,.

We define
\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n}):=\fint_{A_{n}}\left(\nabla u_{n}-\nabla^{s}u_{n}\right)

and
u_{n,\bot}(x):=u_{n}(x)-\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\,x

with
\nabla^{s}u_{n,\bot}=\nabla^{s}u_{n}
. Hence by (
2.18
)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u_{n}-\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left\|\nabla^{s}u_{n,\bot}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}=C_{p}\left\|\nabla^{s}u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\,.

We directly infer with
C_{n}:=\frac{\left|A_{n}\right|}{\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|r_{n}^{d}}

C_{p}C_{n}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}+\left\|\nabla u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\right)}}\right)\to 0\,,\qquad C_{n}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|\nabla u_{n}-\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\to 0\,.

(2.21)

Furthermore, we find

\displaystyle 1=\left\|\nabla u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}-\left\|\nabla u_{n}-\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\,,

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\nabla u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}-\left\|\nabla u_{n}-\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\,,

and hence
\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\to 1

due to (
2.21
). Since
\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})

are constant, it holds

C_{n}\,\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\right)}}^{p}=\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}^{p}

and we infer from a similar calculation

\displaystyle C_{n}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\left\|\nabla u_{n}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\right)}}+\left\|\nabla u_{n}-\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq C_{n}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r_{n}}{\left(x_{n}\right)}\right)}}
