\displaystyle\geq\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\,.

This implies
\left\|\overline{\nabla_{n}^{\bot}}(u_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(A_{n}\right)}}\to 0

by (
2.21
), a contradiction. Hence, (
2.19
)
holds with
\tilde{C}_{p}=nC_{p}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Estimate (
2.20
) now follows from (
2.19
)
and (
2.18
) and the definition of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)})
.
∎

2.6
Korn-Poincaré Inequalities

Generalizing the above Korn inequality to a Korn-Poincaré inequality,
we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{(0),\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}

\displaystyle:=\left\{u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)})\,:\;\exists x:\,B_{r}(x)\subset\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\;\vee\right.\phantom{:=W^{1,p}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}}\quad}

\displaystyle\phantom{:=\left\{u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)})\,:\right\}}\left.\int_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}}u_{i}=0\;\vee\;\forall i,j:\,\int_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}}\partial_{i}u_{j}-\partial_{j}u_{i}=0\right\}\,.

Lemma 2.17

(Mixed Korn-Poincaré inequality on balls)
.

For every
p\in[1,\infty)

there exists
C_{p}>0
such that for every
R>0
,
r\in(0,R)
and
every
x\in\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}
with
\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}
it holds

\displaystyle\forall u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{(0),\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{d}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}\,,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{2d-1}\left(1+\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{1-p}\right)R^{p}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}\,.

(2.23)

Proof.

Apply Lemma
2.16
for
R=1
and use a simple scaling
argument to obtain

\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}\leq C_{p}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{d}\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}\right)\,.

Afterwards apply Lemma
2.10
.
∎

Lemma 2.18

(Mixed Korn-Poincaré inequality on cylinders)
.

For every
p\in[1,\infty)
and
r>0

there exists
C_{p}>0
such that the following holds: Let
r<L
,

P_{L,r}:=(0,L)\times\mathbb{B}_{r}^{d-1}{\left(0\right)}
and
x\in P_{L,r}
such that

\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)}\subset P_{L,r}
then for every
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(P_{L,r})

\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(\left(\frac{L}{r}\right)^{p}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}+\frac{L}{r}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)})}^{p}\right)\,.

(2.24)

Furthermore,

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(\frac{L^{2p}}{r^{p}}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}+\frac{L^{p+1}}{r}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)})}^{p}+\frac{L}{r}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(x\right)})}^{p}\right)\,,

(2.25)

and if additionally
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{(0),\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}(P_{L,r})

then

\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\frac{L^{p}}{r^{p}}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\,,\quad\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\leq C_{p}\frac{L^{2p}}{r^{p}}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(P_{L,r})}^{p}\,,

(2.26)

Defining
\overline{\nabla_{a,\delta}^{\bot}}u:=\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}}\left(\nabla u-\nabla^{s}u\right)

and

\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right](x):=\overline{\nabla_{a,\delta}^{\bot}}u{\left(x-a\right)}+\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}}u

(2.27)

we find for
a,b
with
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)},\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(b\right)}\subset P_{L,r}

for every
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}^{1,p}(P_{L,r})
that

\left|\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right](x)-\left[\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right](x)\right|^{p}\leq C\left|x-a\right|^{p}\frac{\left|a-b\right|^{2p}}{\delta^{p+d}}\left(\int_{\mathrm{conv}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}\cup\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(b\right)}\right)}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)\,.

(2.28)

Furthermore, for every
\delta<r
we find

\left|\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},r}u\right](x)-\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right](x)\right|^{p}\leq C\left(\left(\frac{\delta}{r}\right)^{-d}\left|x-a\right|^{p}+\left(\frac{\delta}{r}\right)^{1-d}(1+\left(\frac{\delta}{r}\right)^{p-d})\right)r^{p-d}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(a\right)}\right)}}^{p}\,.

(2.29)

Proof.

Step1:
W.l.o.g we assume
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
a=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}
,

b=(L-\frac{1}{2})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}
,
r=\frac{1}{2}
and define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k}

\displaystyle:=\left(k\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}+[0,1)\times\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}^{d-1}{\left(0\right)}\right)\,,\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k}:=k\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}+\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right)}

\displaystyle\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)

\displaystyle:=\left[\mathcal{M}_{\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s},\frac{1}{2}}u\right](x)=\left[\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k}}\left(\nabla u-\nabla^{s}u\right)\right]x+\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k}}u\,.

Then we find by Lemma
2.16

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\left(u-\tau_{K}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}+\left\|\nabla\tau_{K}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}+\left\|\nabla\tau_{K}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}\right)\,.

Since
\nabla\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u
is constant, we find

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\tau_{K}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\tau_{0}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{0})}^{p}+C\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left\|\nabla\left(\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{1}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k+1}\right)}}\right)^{p}\,.

Furthermore, we find

\displaystyle\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}{\left(u-\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)}

\displaystyle=\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k}}\left(\nabla u-\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k+1}}\left(\nabla u-\nabla^{s}u\right)-\nabla^{s}u\right)x+\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k}}\left(u-\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k+1}}u\right)

\displaystyle=\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}u=\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}{\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)}\,.

This implies by
\nabla\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)=\fint_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k+1}}\left(\nabla-\nabla^{s}\right)\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)

and Lemma
2.16
and Theorem
2.12

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\left(\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k+1}\right)}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k+1}\right)}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k+1}\right)}}^{p}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{lem:General-Korn}}}{{\leq}}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k+1}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k}\right)}}^{p}+\left\|\nabla\left(u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{k}\right)}}^{p}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{thm:Duran-Korn}}}{{\leq}}C\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k+1}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k}\right)}}^{p}\,.

Since the last inequality implies

\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left\|\nabla\left(\tau_{k+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\tau_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right\|_{L^{1}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{k+1}\right)}}\right)^{p}\leq K^{p-1}C\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left((0,K)\times\mathbb{B}_{1}^{d-1}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}

and
\left\|\nabla\tau_{0}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{0})}^{p}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{0})}^{p}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{0})}^{p}\right)

by Lemma
2.16
we find in total

\left\|\nabla\tau_{K}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{K})}^{p}\leq C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}_{0})}^{p}+CK^{p-1}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left((0,K)\times\mathbb{B}_{1}^{d-1}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}\,.

Adding the last inequality from
K=0
to
K=L
implies (
2.24
)
through scaling. Applying Corollary
2.11
we
infer that (
2.25
) and (
2.26
).

Step 2: We observe that Step 1 also holds for
P_{L,r}
being replaced
by
\mathrm{conv}(\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}\cup\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(b\right)})
. Writing
u_{b}:=u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u

we find from the above calculations

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right|^{p}(x)

\displaystyle=\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}\left(u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right)\right|^{p}(x)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\left(\left|x-a\right|^{p}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}}\left|\nabla u_{b}-\nabla^{s}u_{b}\right|^{p}+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}}\left|u_{b}\right|^{p}\right)\,.

Using that
u_{b}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{(0),\nabla^{\bot}(0),r}^{1,p}(\mathrm{conv}(\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(a\right)}\cup\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(b\right)}))
,
we find (
2.28
) with help of (
2.26
)
and Lemma
2.17
.

Step 3: W.l.o.g.
a=0
. Writing
\bar{u}(y):=u(y)-{\left(\overline{\nabla_{a,\delta}^{\bot}}u\right)}\,y

with
\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}}u=\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(0\right)}}\overline{u}
we infer (
2.29
)
from Lemmas
2.16
and
2.10
via

\displaystyle\left|\left[\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathfrak{s},1}u\right](x)-\left[\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta}u\right](x)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}}\nabla u-\nabla^{s}u-\overline{\nabla_{a,\delta}^{\bot}}u\right|^{p}\left|x\right|^{p}+\left|\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}}\overline{u}-\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}}\overline{u}\right|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}}\left(\left|\nabla u-\overline{\nabla_{a,\delta}^{\bot}}u\right|^{p}+\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)\left|x\right|^{p}+\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}}\left|\overline{u}-\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(0\right)}}\overline{u}\right|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\delta^{-d}\left|x\right|^{p}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}+\delta^{1-d}\left(1+\delta^{p-d}\right)\left\|\nabla\overline{u}\right\|_{L^{p}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}\right)}}^{p}\right)\,.

∎

2.7
Voronoi Tessellations and Delaunay Triangulation

Definition 2.19

(Voronoi Tessellation)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}=\left(x_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence
of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
x_{i}\neq x_{k}
if
i\neq k
. For each

x\in\mathbb{X}
let

G{\left(x\right)}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\forall\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{X}\backslash\left\{x\right\}:\,\left|x-y\right|<\left|\tilde{x}-y\right|\right\}\,.

Then
\left(G{\left(x_{i}\right)}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is called the
Voronoi
tessellation
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
w.r.t.
\mathbb{X}
. For each
x\in\mathbb{X}
we define

d{\left(x\right)}:=\mathrm{diam}G{\left(x\right)}
.

We will need the following result on Voronoi tessellation of a minimal
diameter.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
and let
\mathbb{X}=\left(x_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

be a sequence of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left|x_{i}-x_{k}\right|>2\mathfrak{r}

if
i\neq k
. For
x\in\mathbb{X}
let
\mathcal{I}{\left(x\right)}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{X}\,:\;G{\left(y\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G{\left(x\right)}\right)}\not=\emptyset\right\}
.
Then
y\in\mathcal{I}(x)
implies
\left|x-y\right|\leq 4d(x)
and

\#\mathcal{I}{\left(x\right)}\leq\left(\frac{4d{\left(x\right)}}{\mathfrak{r}}\right)^{d}\,.

(2.30)

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{X}_{k}=\left\{x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\,:\;\mathcal{H}^{d-1}{\left(\partial G_{k}\cap\partial G_{j}\right)}\geq 0\right\}

the neighbors of
x_{k}
and
d_{k}:=d{\left(x_{k}\right)}
. Then all
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}

satisfy
\left|x_{k}-x_{j}\right|\leq 2d_{k}
. Moreover, every
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{X}

with
\left|\text{$\tilde{x}$}-x_{k}\right|>4d_{k}
has
the property that
\mathrm{dist}{\left(\,\partial G\left(\tilde{x}\right),\,x_{k}\,\right)}>2d_{k}>d_{k}+\mathfrak{r}

and
\tilde{x}\not\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
. Since every Voronoi cell contains
a ball of radius
\mathfrak{r}
, this implies that
\#\mathcal{I}_{k}\leq\left|\mathbb{B}_{4d_{k}}{\left(x_{k}\right)}\right|/\left|\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(0\right)}\right|=\left(\frac{4d_{k}}{\mathfrak{r}}\right)^{d}
.
∎

Definition 2.21

(Delaunay Triangulation)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}=\left(x_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence
of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
x_{i}\neq x_{k}
if
i\neq k
. The Delaunay
triangulation is the dual unoriented graph (see Def.

below) of the Voronoi tessellation, i.e. we say
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{X}):=\left\{(x,y):\;\mathcal{H}^{d-1}{\left(\partial G(x)\cap\partial G(y)\right)}\neq 0\right\}
.

2.8
Local
\eta
-Regularity

Figure 1:
An illustration of
\eta
-regularity. In Theorem
2.25

we will rely on a “gray” region
like in this picture.

Definition 2.22

(
\eta
- regularity)
.

For a function
\eta:\,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\to(0,r]
we call
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

\eta
-regular
if

\forall p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}},\,\varepsilon\in{\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)}\,,\,\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon\eta(p)}{\left(p\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}:\,\eta(\tilde{p})>(1-\varepsilon)\eta(p)\,.

(2.31)

Remark 2.23
.

This concept and its consequences from Lemma
2.24

and Theorem
2.25
will be extensively used
later to cover
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
by a suitable family of open balls.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a locally
\eta
-regular
set for
\eta:\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\to(0,\mathfrak{r})
. Then
\eta:\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\to\mathbb{R}
is
locally Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
1
and for
every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon\eta}{\left(p\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

it holds

\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}\eta{\left(p\right)}>\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}>\eta{\left(p\right)}-\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|>\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\eta{\left(p\right)}\,.

(2.32)

Furthermore,

\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|\leq\varepsilon\max\left\{\eta{\left(p\right)},\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\right\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\min\left\{\eta{\left(p\right)},\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\right\}

(2.33)

Proof.

Let
p,\tilde{p}
such that
\left|\tilde{p}-p\right|<\frac{1}{2}\eta(p)

with
\varepsilon_{p,\tilde{p}}:=\inf\left\{\varepsilon:\,\left|\tilde{p}-p\right|<\varepsilon\eta(p)\right\}
.
This means
\varepsilon\in[\varepsilon_{p,\tilde{p}},\frac{1}{2})
iff
\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\geq\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\eta(p)

and we find

\displaystyle\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\eta{\left(p\right)}-\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|=\eta{\left(p\right)}-\varepsilon_{p,\tilde{p}}\eta{\left(p\right)}>\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\eta{\left(p\right)}

which implies
\left|\tilde{p}-p\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}

and the local Lipschitz continuity by a symmetry argument in
p
,

\tilde{p}
. This in turn leads to
\eta{\left(p\right)}>\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}

or

\eta{\left(p\right)}=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\eta{\left(p\right)}<\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\left(\eta{\left(p\right)}-\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|\right)<\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{1-2\varepsilon}\eta{\left(p\right)}\,,

implying (
2.32
) and continuity of
\eta
.

In order to prove (
2.33
), w.l.o.g.
let
\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\leq\eta{\left(p\right)}
. Then

\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|\leq\varepsilon\eta{\left(p\right)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\,.

∎

Theorem 2.25
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a closed
set and let
\eta{\left(\cdot\right)}\in C{\left(\Gamma\right)}
be bounded and satisfy
for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and for
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\varepsilon\eta{\left(p\right)}

\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}\,\eta{\left(p\right)}>\eta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}>\eta{\left(p\right)}-\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|>\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\eta{\left(p\right)}\,.

(2.34)

and define
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p\right)}=2^{-K}\eta{\left(p\right)}
,
K\geq 2
. Then for
every
C\in(0,1)
there exists a locally finite covering of
\Gamma

with balls
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{k}\right)}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}
for a countable
number of points
{\left(p_{k}\right)}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Gamma
such that for
every
i\neq k
with
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{i}\right)}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{k}\right)}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\neq\emptyset

it holds

\displaystyle\frac{2^{K-1}-1}{2^{K-1}}\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{i}\right)}\leq\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\leq\frac{2^{K-1}}{2^{K-1}-1}\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{i}\right)}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{K}-1}{2^{K-1}-1}\min{\left\{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{i}\right)},\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\right\}}\geq\left|p_{i}-p_{k}\right|\geq C\max{\left\{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{i}\right)},\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\right\}}

Proof.

We chose
\delta>0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\left(1-\delta\right)>C
.
W.o.l.g. assume
\tilde{\eta}<(1-\delta)
. Consider
\tilde{Q}:=\left[0,\frac{1}{n}\right]^{d}
,
let
q_{1,\dots,n^{d}}
denote the
n^{d}
elements of
[0,1)^{d}\cap\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{d}}{n}

and let
\tilde{Q}_{z,i}=\tilde{Q}+z+q_{i}
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. We set
B_{(0)}:=\emptyset
,

\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma
,
\eta_{k}:=\left(1-\delta\right)^{k}
and for

k\geq 1
we construct the covering using inductively defined open
sets
B_{(k)}
and closed set
\Gamma_{k}
as follows:

1.

Define
\Gamma_{k,1}=\Gamma_{k}
. For
i=1,\dots,n^{d}
do the following:

(a)

For every
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
do

\displaystyle\text{if }\exists p\in\left(\eta_{k}\tilde{Q}_{z,i}\right)\cap\Gamma_{k,i},\,\tilde{\eta}{\left(p\right)}\in(\eta_{k},\eta_{k-1}]

\displaystyle\text{then set }

\displaystyle b_{z,i}=\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}\,,\;\mathbb{X}_{z,i}=\left\{p\right\}

\displaystyle\text{otherwise }

\displaystyle\text{set }

\displaystyle b_{z,i}=\emptyset\,,\;\mathbb{X}_{z,i}=\emptyset\,.

(b)

Define
B_{(k),i}:=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}b_{z,i}
and
\Gamma_{k,i+1}=\Gamma_{k,i}\backslash B_{(k),i}

and
\mathbb{X}_{(k),i}:=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathbb{X}_{z,i}
.

Observe:
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{X}_{(k),i}
implies
\left|p_{1}-p_{2}\right|>\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\eta_{k}

and
p_{3}\in\mathbb{X}_{(k),j}
,
j<i
implies
p_{1}\not\in\mathbb{B}_{\eta_{k}}{\left(p_{3}\right)}

and hence
\left|p_{1}-p_{3}\right|>\eta_{k}
. Similar,
p_{3}\in\mathbb{X}_{l}
,

l<k
, implies
\left|p_{1}-p_{3}\right|>\eta_{l}>\eta_{k}
.

2.

Define
\Gamma_{k+1}:=\Gamma_{k,n^{d}+1}
,
\mathbb{X}_{k}:=\bigcup_{i}\mathbb{X}_{(k),i}
.

The above covering of
\Gamma
is complete in the sense that every

x\in\Gamma
lies in one of the balls (by contradiction). We denote

\mathbb{X}:=\bigcup_{k}\mathbb{X}_{k}=\left(p_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the family of
centers of the above constructed covering of
\Gamma
and find the
following properties: Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
be such that
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}}{\left(p_{1}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}}{\left(p_{2}\right)}\neq\emptyset
.
W.l.o.g. let
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}\geq\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}
.
Then the following two properties are satisfied due to (
2.34
)

1.

It holds
\left|p_{1}-p_{2}\right|\leq 2\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{2^{K-1}}\eta{\left(p_{1}\right)}

and hence
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}}{\left(p_{2}\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{2^{2-K}\eta{\left(p_{1}\right)}}{\left(p_{1}\right)}

and
\eta{\left(p_{2}\right)}\geq\frac{2^{K-1}-1}{2^{K-1}}\eta{\left(p_{1}\right)}
.
Furthermore
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}\geq\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}\geq\frac{2^{K-1}-1}{2^{K-1}}\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}
.

2.

Let
k
such that
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}\in\left(\eta_{k},\eta_{k+1}\right]
.
If also
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}\in\left(\eta_{k},\eta_{k+1}\right]

then the observation in Step 1.(b) implies
\left|p_{1}-p_{2}\right|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\eta_{k}\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\left(1-\delta\right)\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}
.
If
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}\not\in\left[\eta_{k},\eta_{k+1}\right)

then
\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{2}\right)}<\eta_{k}
and hence
p_{2}\not\in\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}}{\left(p_{1}\right)}
,
implying
\left|p_{1}-p_{2}\right|>\tilde{\eta}{\left(p_{1}\right)}
.

Due to our choice of
n
and
\delta
, this concludes the proof.
∎

2.9
Dynamical Systems

Assumption 2.26
.

Throughout this work we assume that
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})

is a probability space with countably generated
\sigma
-algebra

\mathscr{F}
.

Due to the insight in
[
10
]
, shortly sketched
in the next two subsections, after a measurable transformation the
probability space
\Omega
can be assumed to be metric and separable,
which always ensures Assumption
2.26
.

Definition 2.27

(Dynamical system)
.

A dynamical system
on
\Omega
is a family
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
of measurable bijective
mappings
\tau_{x}:\Omega\mapsto\Omega
satisfying (i)-(iii):

(i)

\tau_{x}\circ\tau_{y}=\tau_{x+y}
,
\tau_{0}=id
(Group
property)

(ii)

\mathbb{P}(\tau_{-x}B)=\mathbb{P}(B)\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\,B\in\mathscr{F}
(Measure
preserving)

(iii)

A:\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega\rightarrow\Omega\qquad(x,\omega)\mapsto\tau_{x}\omega

is measurable (Measurability of evaluation)

A set
A\subset\Omega
is almost invariant if
\mathbb{P}\left(\left(A\cup\tau_{x}A\right)\backslash\left(A\cap\tau_{x}A\right)\right)=0
.
The family

\mathscr{I}=\left\{A\in\mathscr{F}\,:\;\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\left(A\cup\tau_{x}A\right)\backslash\left(A\cap\tau_{x}A\right)\right)=0\right\}

(2.36)

of almost invariant sets is
\sigma
-algebra and

\mathbb{E}\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)\text{denotes the expectation of }f:\,\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\text{ w.r.t. }\mathscr{I}\,.

(2.37)

A concept linked to dynamical systems is the concept of stationarity.

Definition 2.28

(Stationary)
.

Let

X
be a measurable space and let
f:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to X
. Then

f
is called (weakly) stationary if
f(\omega,x)=f(\tau_{x}\omega,0)

for (almost) every
x
.

Definition 2.29
.

A
family
\left(A_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called convex averaging
sequence if

(i)

each
A_{n}
is convex

(ii)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
holds
A_{n}\subset A_{n+1}

(iii)

there exists a sequence
r_{n}
with
r_{n}\to\infty
as

n\to\infty
such that
B_{r_{n}}(0)\subseteq A_{n}
.

We sometimes may take the following stronger assumption.

Definition 2.30
.

A convex averaging sequence
A_{n}
is called regular if

\left|A_{n}\right|^{-1}\#\left\{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,:\;\left(z+\mathbb{T}\right)\cap\partial A_{n}\neq\emptyset\right\}\to 0\,.

The latter condition is evidently fulfilled for sequences of cones
or balls. Convex averaging sequences are important in the context
of ergodic theorems.

Theorem 2.31

(Ergodic Theorem
[
4
]
Theorems 10.2.II and also
[
27
]
)
.

Let

\left(A_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex averaging sequence,
let
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be a dynamical system on
\Omega
with
invariant
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{I}
and let
f:\,\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable
with
\left|\mathbb{E}(f)\right|<\infty
. Then for almost all
\omega\in\Omega

\left|A_{n}\right|^{-1}\int_{A_{n}}f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\,\mathrm{d}x\to\mathbb{E}{\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)}\,.

(2.38)

We observe that
\mathbb{E}\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)
is of particular importance.
For the calculations in this work, we will particularly focus on the
case of trivial
\mathscr{I}
. This is called ergodicity, as we will explain
in the following.

Definition 2.32

(Ergodicity and mixing)
.

A
dynamical system
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})

is called
mixing
if for every measurable
A,B\subset\Omega

it holds

\lim_{\left\|x\right\|\to\infty}\mathbb{P}{\left(A\cap\tau_{x}B\right)}=\mathbb{P}(A)\,\mathbb{P}(B)\,.

(2.39)

A dynamical system is called
ergodic
if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left(2n\right)^{d}}\int_{[-n,n]^{d}}\mathbb{P}{\left(A\cap\tau_{x}B\right)}\mathrm{d}x=\mathbb{P}(A)\,\mathbb{P}(B)\,.

(2.40)

Remark 2.33
.

a) Let
\Omega=\left\{\omega_{0}=0\right\}

with the trivial
\sigma
-algebra and
\tau_{x}\omega_{0}=\omega_{0}
.
Then
\tau
is evidently mixing. However, the realizations are constant
functions
f_{\omega}(x)=c
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for some constant
c
.

b) A typical ergodic system is given by
\Omega=\mathbb{T}
with the Lebesgue

\sigma
-algebra and
\mathbb{P}=\mathcal{L}
the Lebesgue measure. The dynamical
system is given by
\tau_{x}y:=\left(x+y\right)\!\mod\mathbb{T}
.

c) It is known that
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is ergodic if and only
if every almost invariant set
A\in\mathscr{I}
has probability
\mathbb{P}(A)\in\left\{0,1\right\}

(see
[
4
]
Proposition 10.3.III) i.e.

\left[\,\,\forall x\,\mathbb{P}{\left(\left(\tau_{x}A\cup A\right)\backslash\left(\tau_{x}A\cap A\right)\right)}=0\,\,\right]\;\Rightarrow\;\mathbb{P}{\left(A\right)}\in\left\{0,1\right\}\,.

(2.41)

d) It is sufficient to show (
2.39
) or (
2.40
)
for
A
and
B
in a ring that generates the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{F}
.
We refer to
[
4
]
, Section 10.2, for the later results.

A further useful property of ergodic dynamical systems, which we will
use below, is the following:

Lemma 2.34

(Ergodic times mixing is ergodic)
.

Let
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})

and
(\hat{\Omega},\hat{\mathscr{F}},\hat{\mathbb{P}})
be probability spaces with
dynamical systems
(\tilde{\tau}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
(\hat{\tau}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}

respectively. Let
\Omega:=\tilde{\Omega}\times\hat{\Omega}
be the
usual product measure space with the notation
\omega=(\tilde{\omega},\hat{\omega})\in\Omega

for
\tilde{\omega}\in\tilde{\Omega}
and
\hat{\omega}\in\hat{\Omega}
.
If
\tilde{\tau}
is ergodic and
\hat{\tau}
is mixing, then
\tau_{x}(\tilde{\omega},\hat{\omega}):=(\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{\omega},\hat{\tau}_{x}\hat{\omega})

is ergodic.

Proof.

Relying on Remark
2.33
.c) we verify (
2.40
)
by proving it for sets
A=\tilde{A}\times\hat{A}
and
B=\tilde{B}\times\hat{B}

which generate
\mathscr{F}:=\tilde{\mathscr{F}}\otimes\hat{\mathscr{F}}
. We make use of

A\cap B=\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{B}\right)\times\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)

and observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{\left(A\cap\tau_{x}B\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}{\left(\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{B}\right)\times\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{\tau}_{x}\hat{B}\right)\right)}=\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{\tau}_{x}\hat{B}\right)}\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{B}\right)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)}\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{B}\right)}+\left[\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{\tau}_{x}\hat{B}\right)}-\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)}\right]\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{B}\right)}\,.

Using ergodicity, we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left(2n\right)^{d}}\int_{[-n,n]^{d}}\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)}\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{B}\right)}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)\right)}\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{B}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}{\left(A\cap B\right)}\,.

(2.42)

Since
\hat{\tau}
is mixing, we find for every
\varepsilon>0
some
R>0

such that
\left\|x\right\|>R
implies
\left|\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{\tau}_{x}\hat{B}\right)}-\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)}\right|<\varepsilon
.
For
n>R
we find

\frac{1}{\left(2n\right)^{d}}\int_{[-n,n]^{d}}\left|\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{\tau}_{x}\hat{B}\right)}-\hat{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\hat{A}\cap\hat{B}\right)}\right|\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}{\left(\tilde{A}\cap\tilde{\tau}_{x}\tilde{B}\right)}\\
\leq\frac{1}{\left(2n\right)^{d}}\int_{[-n,n]^{d}}\varepsilon+\frac{1}{\left(2n\right)^{d}}\int_{[-R,R]^{d}}2\to\varepsilon\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\,.

(2.43)

The last two limits (
2.42
) and
(
2.43
) imply (
2.40
).
∎

Remark 2.35
.

The above proof heavily relies on the mixing property of
\hat{\tau}
.
Note that for
\hat{\tau}
being only ergodic, the statement is wrong,
as can be seen from the product of two periodic processes in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}

(see Remark
2.33
). Here, the invariant sets are
given by
I_{A}:=\left\{\left(\left(y+x\right)\!\mod\mathbb{T}\,,\,x\right)\,:\;y\in A\right\}

for arbitrary measurable
A\subset\mathbb{T}
.

2.10
Random Measures and Palm Theory

We recall some facts from random measure theory (see
[
4
]
)
which will be needed for homogenization. Let
\mathfrak{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote
the space of locally bounded Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e. bounded
on every bounded Borel-measurable set) equipped with the Vague topology,
which is generated by the sets

\left\{\mu\,:\;\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu\in A\right\}\text{ for every open }A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }f\in C_{c}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}\,.

This topology is metrizable, complete and countably generated. A random
measure is a measurable mapping

\mu_{\bullet}:\;\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,,\qquad\omega\mapsto\mu_{\omega}

which is equivalent to both of the following conditions

1.

For every bounded Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the map
\omega\mapsto\mu_{\omega}(A)

is measurable

2.

For every
f\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the map
\omega\mapsto\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}

is measurable.

A random measure is stationary if the distribution of
\mu_{\omega}(A)

is invariant under translations of
A
that is
\mu_{\omega}(A)

and
\mu_{\omega}(A+x)
share the same distribution. From stationarity
of
\mu_{\omega}
one concludes the existence (
[
10
,
22
]

and references therein) of a dynamical system
\left(\tau_{x}\right)_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}

on
\Omega
such that
\mu_{\omega}\left(A+x\right)=\mu_{\tau_{x}\omega}\left(A\right)
.
By a deep theorem due to Mecke (see
[
19
,
4
]
) the
measure

\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)=\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(s)\,\chi_{A}(\tau_{s}\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

can be defined on
\Omega
for every positive
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

with compact support.
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}
is independent from
g
and in case

\mu_{\omega}=\mathcal{L}
we find
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathbb{P}
. Furthermore, for
every
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
-measurable non negative
or
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}\times\mathcal{L}
- integrable functions
f
the Campbell
formula

\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x,\tau_{x}\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Omega}f(x,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}x

holds. The measure
\mu_{\omega}
has finite intensity if
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega)<+\infty
.

We denote by

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\mathcal{P}}}{\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)}:=\int_{\Omega}f\text{ the expectation of }f\text{ w.r.t. the }\sigma\text{-algebra }\mathscr{I}\text{ and }\mu_{\mathcal{P}}\,.

(2.44)

For random measures we find a more general version of Theorem
2.31
.

Theorem 2.36

(Ergodic Theorem
[
4
]
12.2.VIII)
.

Let
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}\right)

be a probability space,
\left(A_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
a convex averaging sequence, let
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be a dynamical
system on
\Omega
with invariant
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{I}
and let

f:\,\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable with
\int_{\Omega}\left|f\right|\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}<\infty
.
Then for
\mathbb{P}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega

\left|A_{n}\right|^{-1}\int_{A_{n}}f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}(x)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\mathcal{P}}}{\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)}\,.

(2.45)

Given a bounded open (and convex) set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\Omega
, it is not
hard to see that the following generalization holds:

Theorem 2.37

(General Ergodic Theorem)
.

Let
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}\right)

be a probability space,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set with

0\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
, let
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be a dynamical system on
\Omega

with invariant
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{I}
and let
f:\,\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

be measurable with
\int_{\Omega}\left|f\right|\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}<\infty
.
Then for
\mathbb{P}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
it holds

\forall\varphi\in C_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}):\quad n^{-d}\int_{n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi(\frac{x}{n})f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}(x)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\mathcal{P}}}{\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi\,.

(2.46)

Sketch of proof.

Chose a countable dense family of functions
\varphi\in C_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

that spans
L^{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})
and that have support on a ball. Use a Cantor
argument and Theorem
2.36
to prove
the statement for a countable dense family of
C_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})
. From
here, we conclude by density.

The last result can be used to prove the most general ergodic theorem
which we will use in this work:
∎

Theorem 2.38

(General Ergodic Theorem for the Lebesgue measure)
.

Let
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}\right)

be a probability space,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set with

0\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
, let
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be a dynamical system on
\Omega

with invariant
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{I}
and let
f\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mu_{\mathcal{P}})

and
\varphi\in L^{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})
, where
1<p,q<\infty
,
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Then for
\mathbb{P}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
it holds

n^{-d}\int_{n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi(\frac{x}{n})f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\,\mathrm{d}x\to\mathbb{E}{\left(f\right)}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi\,.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{\delta}\in C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}})
with
\left\|\varphi-\varphi_{\delta}\right\|_{L^{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}<\delta
.
Then

\displaystyle\left|n^{-d}\int_{n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi(\frac{x}{n})f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\,\mathrm{d}x-\mathbb{E}{\left(f\right)}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\left\|\varphi-\varphi_{\delta}\right\|_{L^{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\left(n^{-d}\int_{n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+\left|n^{-d}\int_{n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi_{\delta}(x)f{\left(\tau_{x}\omega\right)}\,\mathrm{d}x-\mathbb{E}{\left(f\right)}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\varphi_{\delta}\right|+\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\mathcal{P}}}{\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\varphi-\varphi_{\delta}\right|\,,

which implies the claim.
∎

2.11
Random Sets

The theory of random measures and the theory of random geometry are
closely related. In what follows, we recapitulate those results that
are important in the context of the theory developed below and shed
some light on the correlations between random sets and random measures.

Let
\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the set of all closed sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{V}:=

\displaystyle\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;F\cap V\not=\emptyset\right\}

\displaystyle\mbox{if }V\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\textnormal{is an open set}\,,

(2.47)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{K}:=

\displaystyle\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;F\cap K=\emptyset\right\}

\displaystyle\mbox{if }K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\textnormal{is a compact set}\,.

(2.48)

The
Fell-topology

\mathscr{T}_{F}
is created by all sets
\mathfrak{F}_{V}

and
\mathfrak{F}^{K}
and the topological space
(\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathscr{T}_{F})

is compact, Hausdorff and separable
[
18
]
.

Remark 2.39
.

We find for closed sets
F_{n},F
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

that
F_{n}\to F
if and only if
[
18
]

1.

for every
x\in F
there exists
x_{n}\in F_{n}
such that
x=\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}

and

2.

if
F_{n_{k}}
is a subsequence, then every convergent sequence
x_{n_{k}}

with
x_{n_{k}}\in F_{n_{k}}
satisfies
\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{n_{k}}\in F
.

If we restrict the Fell-topology to the compact sets
\mathfrak{K}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

it is equivalent with the Hausdorff topology given by the Hausdorff
distance

\mathrm{d}{\left(A,B\right)}=\max\left\{\sup_{y\in B}\inf_{x\in A}\left|x-y\right|\,,\,\,\sup_{x\in A}\inf_{y\in B}\left|x-y\right|\right\}\,.

Remark 2.40
.

For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
closed, the set

\mathfrak{F}(A):=\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;F\subset A\right\}

is a closed subspace of
\mathfrak{F}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}
. This holds since

\mathfrak{F}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}\backslash\mathfrak{F}{\left(A\right)}=\left\{B\in\mathfrak{F}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}\,:\;B\cap\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash A\right)\not=\emptyset\right\}=\mathfrak{F}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash A}\quad\text{is open.}

.

Lemma 2.41

(Continuity of geometric operations)
.

The maps
\tau_{x}:\,A\mapsto A+x
and

b_{\delta}:\,A\mapsto\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(A\right)}}
are continuous in

\mathfrak{F}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
.

Proof.

We show that preimages of open sets are open. For open sets
V
we
find

\displaystyle\tau_{x}^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{V}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;\tau_{x}F\cap V\not=\emptyset\right\}=\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;F\cap\tau_{-x}V\not=\emptyset\right\}=\mathfrak{F}_{\tau_{-x}V}\,,

\displaystyle b_{\delta}^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{V}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(F\right)}}\cap V\not=\emptyset\right\}=\left\{F\in\mathfrak{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,:\;F\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(V\right)}\not=\emptyset\right\}=\mathfrak{F}_{\left(b_{\delta}V\right)^{\circ}}\,.

The calculations for
\tau_{x}^{-1}{\left(\mathfrak{F}^{K}\right)}=\mathfrak{F}^{\tau_{-x}K}

and
b_{\delta}^{-1}{\left(\mathfrak{F}^{K}\right)}=\mathfrak{F}^{b_{\delta}K}

are analogue.
∎

Remark 2.42
.

The
Matheron-
\sigma
-field

\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}}
is the Borel-
\sigma
-algebra of the Fell-topology
and is fully characterized either by the class
\mathfrak{F}_{V}

of
\mathfrak{F}^{K}
.

Definition 2.43

(Random closed / open set according to Choquet (see
[
18
]

for more details))
.

a)

Let
(\Omega,\sigma,\mathbb{P})
be a probability space. Then a
Random
Closed Set (RACS)
is a measurable mapping

A:(\Omega,\sigma,\mathbb{P})\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{F},\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}})

b)

Let
\tau_{x}
be a dynamical system on
\Omega
. A random
closed set is called stationary if its characteristic functions
\chi_{A(\omega)}

are stationary, i.e. they satisfy
\chi_{A(\omega)}(x)=\chi_{A(\tau_{x}\omega)}(0)

for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Two
random sets are jointly stationary if they can be parameterized by
the same probability space such that they are both stationary.

c)

A random closed set
\Gamma:(\Omega,\sigma,P)\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{F},\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}})\quad\omega\mapsto\Gamma(\omega)

is called a
Random closed
C^{k}
-Manifold
if
\Gamma(\omega)

is a piece-wise
C^{k}
-manifold for P almost every
\omega
.

d)

A measurable mapping

A:(\Omega,\sigma,\mathbb{P})\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{F},\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}})

is called
Random Open Set (RAOS)
if
\omega\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash A(\omega)

is a RACS.

The importance of the concept of random geometries for stochastic
homogenization stems from the following Lemma by Zähle. It states
that every random closed set induces a random measure. Thus, every
stationary RACS induces a stationary random measure.

Lemma 2.44

(
[
32
]
Theorem 2.1.3 resp. Corollary 2.1.5)
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}_{m}\subset\mathfrak{F}

be the space of closed m-dimensional sub manifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such
that the corresponding Hausdorff measure is locally finite. Then,
the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{m}
is
the smallest such that

M_{B}:\mathfrak{F}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad M\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{m}(M\cap B)

is measurable for every measurable and bounded
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

This means that

M_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}:\mathfrak{F}_{m}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad M\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{m}(M\cap\cdot)

is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra created by the
Vague topology on
\mathfrak{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Hence a random closed set always induces
a random measure. Based on Lemma
2.44
and
on Palm-theory, the following useful result was obtained in
[
10
]

(See Lemma 2.14 and Section 3.1 therein). We can thus assume w.l.o.g
that
\Omega
is a separable metric space.

Theorem 2.45
.

Let
(\Omega,\sigma,P)
be a probability
space with an ergodic dynamical system
\tau
. Let
A:(\Omega,\sigma,P)\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{F},\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}})

be a stationary random closed
m
-dimensional
C^{k}
-Manifold.

There exists a separable metric space
\tilde{\Omega}\subset\mathfrak{M}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}

with an ergodic dynamical system
\tilde{\tau}
and a mapping
\tilde{A}:\,(\tilde{\Omega},\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\Omega}},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathfrak{F},\sigma_{\mathfrak{F}})

such that
A
and
\tilde{A}
have the same law and such that
\tilde{A}

still is stationary. Furthermore,
(x,\omega)\mapsto\tau_{x}\omega

is continuous. We identify
\tilde{\Omega}=\Omega
,
\tilde{A}=A

and
\tilde{\tau}=\tau
.

Also the following result will be useful below.

Lemma 2.46
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set. Let
\mathfrak{F}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{F}\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\right)

be such that
\mathfrak{F}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
,
A\mapsto\mu(A)
is continuous.
Then

m:\,\mathfrak{F}\times\mathfrak{F}_{0}\to\mathfrak{M}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}\,,\quad\left(P,B\right)\mapsto\begin{cases}A\mapsto\mu{\left(A\cap B\right)}&B\subset P\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

is measurable.

Proof.

For
f\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we introduce
m_{f}
through

m_{f}:\,\left(P,B\right)\mapsto\begin{cases}\int_{B}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu&B\subset P\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

and observe that
m
is measurable if and only if for every
f\in C_{c}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}

the map
m_{f}
is measurable (see Section
2.10
).
Hence, if we prove the latter property, the lemma is proved.

We assume
f\geq 0
and we show that the mapping
m_{f}
is even
upper continuous. In particular, let
(P_{n},B_{n})\to(P,B)
in
\mathfrak{F}\times\mathfrak{F}_{0}

and assume that
B_{n}\subset P_{n}
for all
n>N_{0}
. Since
\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}

is compact, Remark
2.39
. 2. implies that
B\subset P\cap\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}
.
Furthermore, since
f
has compact support, we find
\left|\int_{B_{n}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu-\int_{B}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu\right|\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\left|\mu{\left(B_{n}\right)}-\mu{\left(B\right)}\right|\to 0
.
On the other hand, if there exists a subsequence such that
B_{n}\not\subset P_{n}

for all
n
, then either
B\not\subset P
and
m_{f}(P_{n},B_{n})=0\to m_{f}(P,B)=0

or
B\subset P
and
0=\lim_{n\to\infty}m_{f}(P_{n},B_{n})\leq\int_{B}f\mathrm{d}\mu=m_{f}(P,B)
.
For
f\leq 0
we obtain lower semicontinuity and for general
f

the map
m_{f}
is the sum of an upper and a lower semicontinuous
map, hence measurable.
∎

2.12
Point Processes

Definition 2.47

((Simple) point processes)
.

A
\mathbb{Z}
-valued random measure
\mu_{\omega}
is called point process.
In what follows, we consider the particular case that for almost every

\omega
there exist points
\left(x_{k}(\omega)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

and values
\left(a_{k}\left(\omega\right)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{Z}

such that

\mu_{\omega}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}a_{k}\delta_{x_{k}(\omega)}\,.

The point process
\mu_{\omega}
is called simple if almost surely
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
it holds
a_{k}\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.

Example 2.48

(Poisson process)
.

A
particular example for a stationary point process is the Poisson point
process
\mu_{\omega}=\mathbb{X}_{\omega}
with intensity
\lambda
. Here,
the probability
\mathbb{P}{\left(\mathbb{X}(A)=n\right)}
to find
n
points in a Borel-set

A
with finite measure is given by a Poisson distribution

\mathbb{P}{\left(\mathbb{X}(A)=n\right)}=e^{-\lambda\left|A\right|}\frac{\lambda^{n}\left|A\right|^{n}}{n!}

(2.49)

with expectation
\mathbb{E}{\left(\mathbb{X}(A)\right)}=\lambda\left|A\right|
. Shift-invariance
of (
2.49
) implies that the Poisson point
process is stationary.

We can use a given random point process to construct further processes.

Example 2.49

(Hard core Matern process)
.

The
hard core Matern process is constructed from a given point process

\mathbb{X}_{\omega}
by mutually erasing all points with the distance to
the nearest neighbor smaller than a given constant
r
. If the original
process
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}
is stationary (ergodic), the resulting hard
core process is stationary (ergodic) respectively.

Example 2.50

(Hard core Poisson–Matern process)
.

If a Matern process is constructed from
a Poisson point process, we call it a Poisson–Matern point process.

Lemma 2.51
.

Let
\mu_{\omega}
be a simple point process
with
a_{k}=1
almost surely for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}=\left(x_{k}(\omega)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

is a random closed set of isolated points with no limit points. On
the other hand, if
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}=\left(x_{k}(\omega)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

is a random closed set that almost surely has no limit points then

\mu_{\omega}
is a point process.

Proof.

Let
\mu_{\omega}
be a point process. For open
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
let

\displaystyle f_{V,R}(x)=\mathrm{dist}{\left(\,x,\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\left(V\cap\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(0\right)}\right)\,\right)}\,,\qquad f_{\delta}^{K}(x)=\max{\left\{\,1-\frac{1}{\delta}\mathrm{dist}{\left(x,K\right)}\,,\,0\,\right\}}\,.

Then
f_{V,R}
is Lipschitz with constant
1
and
f_{\delta}^{K}

is Lipschitz with constant
\frac{1}{\delta}
and support in
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(K\right)}
.
Moreover, since
\mu_{\omega}
is locally bounded, the number of
points
x_{k}
that lie within
\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(K\right)}
is bounded. In particular,
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}^{-1}{\left(\mathfrak{F}_{V}\right)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{R>0}\left\{\omega\,:\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{V,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}>0\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}^{-1}{\left(\mathfrak{F}^{K}\right)}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\delta>0}\left\{\omega\,:\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{\delta}^{K}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}>0\right\}\,,

are measurable. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{V}
and
\mathfrak{F}^{K}
generate
the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathfrak{F}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
, it follows
that
\omega\to\mathbb{X}_{\omega}
is measurable.

In order to prove the opposite direction, let
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}=\left(x_{k}(\omega)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

be a random closed set of points. Since
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}
has almost
surely no limit points the measure
\mu_{\omega}
is locally bounded
almost surely. We prove that
\mu_{\omega}
is a random measure by
showing that

\forall f\in C_{c}{\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)}\,:\qquad F:\,\omega\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}\text{ is measurable.}

For
\delta>0
let
\mu_{\omega}^{\delta}{\left(A\right)}:=\left(\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|\delta^{d}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{L}{\left(A\cap\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(\mathbb{X}_{\omega}\right)}\right)}
.
By Lemmas
2.41
and
2.46

we obtain that
F_{\delta}:\,\omega\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\omega}^{\delta}

are measurable. Moreover, for almost every
\omega
we find
F_{\delta}\left(\omega\right)\to F\left(\omega\right)

uniformly and hence
F
is measurable.
∎

Corollary 2.52
.

A random simple point process
\mu_{\omega}
is stationary iff
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}

is stationary.

Hence we can provide the following definition based on Definition

2.43
.

Definition 2.53
.

A point process
\mu_{\omega}

and a random set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
are jointly stationary if
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and
\mathbb{X}

are jointly stationary.

Lemma 2.54
.

Let
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}=\left(x_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

be a Matern point process from Example
2.49
with distance

r
and let for
\delta<\frac{r}{2}
be
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}(\omega):=\bigcup_{i}\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{i})}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}(\omega)
is a random closed set.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.41
:
\mathbb{X}_{\omega}

is measurable and
\mathbb{X}\mapsto\overline{B_{\delta}(\mathbb{X})}
is continuous.
Hence
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}\left(\omega\right)
is measurable.
∎

2.13
Dynamical Systems on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

Definition 2.55
.

Let
\left(\hat{\Omega},\hat{\mathscr{F}},\hat{\mathbb{P}}\right)

be a probability space. A discrete dynamical system on
\hat{\Omega}

is a family
(\hat{\tau}_{z})_{z\in r\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
of measurable bijective
mappings
\hat{\tau}_{z}:\hat{\Omega}\mapsto\hat{\Omega}
satisfying
(i)-(iii) of Definition
2.27
with
\mathbb{R}^{d}
replaced
by
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. A set
A\subset\hat{\Omega}
is almost invariant if for
every
z\in r\mathbb{Z}^{d}
it holds
\mathbb{P}\left(\left(A\cup\hat{\tau}_{z}A\right)\backslash\left(A\cap\hat{\tau}_{z}A\right)\right)=0

and
\hat{\tau}
is called ergodic w.r.t.
r\mathbb{Z}^{d}
if every almost
invariant set has measure
0
or
1
.

Similar to the continuous dynamical systems, also in this discrete
setting an ergodic theorem can be proved.

Theorem 2.56

(See Krengel and Tempel’man
[
16
,
27
]
)
.

Let
\left(A_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

be a convex averaging sequence, let
(\hat{\tau}_{z})_{z\in r\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

be a dynamical system on
\hat{\Omega}
with invariant
\sigma
-algebra

\mathscr{I}
and let
f:\,\hat{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable with
\left|\mathbb{E}(f)\right|<\infty
.
Then for almost all
\hat{\omega}\in\hat{\Omega}

\left|A_{n}\right|^{-1}\sum_{z\in A_{n}\cap r\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f{\left(\hat{\tau}_{z}\hat{\omega}\right)}\to r^{-d}\mathbb{E}{\left(f|\mathscr{I}\right)}\,.

(2.50)

In the following, we restrict to
r=1
for simplicity of notation.

Let
\Omega_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We consider an enumeration
\left(\xi_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
\hat{\Omega}:=\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}=\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}

and write
\hat{\omega}=\left(\hat{\omega}_{\xi_{1}},\hat{\omega}_{\xi_{2}},\dots\right)=\left(\hat{\omega}_{1},\hat{\omega}_{2},\dots\right)

for all
\hat{\omega}\in\hat{\Omega}
. We define a metric on
\hat{\Omega}

through

d(\hat{\omega}_{1},\hat{\omega}_{2})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\frac{\left|\hat{\omega}_{1,\xi_{k}}-\hat{\omega}_{2,\xi_{k}}\right|}{1+\left|\hat{\omega}_{1,\xi_{k}}-\hat{\omega}_{2,\xi_{k}}\right|}\,.

We write
\Omega_{n}:=\Omega_{0}^{n}
and
\mathbb{N}_{n}:=\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\,k\geq n+1\right\}
.
The topology of
\hat{\Omega}
is generated by the open sets
A\times\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{n}}
,
where for some
n>0
,
A\subset\Omega_{n}
is an open set. In case

\Omega_{0}
is compact, the space
\hat{\Omega}
is compact. Further,

\hat{\Omega}
is separable in any case since
\Omega_{0}
is separable
(see
[
14
]
).

Lemma 2.57
.

Suppose for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}

on
\Omega_{n}
such that for every measurable
A_{n}\subset\Omega_{n}

it holds
\mathbb{P}_{n+k}{\left(A_{n}\times\Omega^{k}\right)}=\mathbb{P}_{n}{\left(A_{n}\right)}
. Then

\mathbb{P}
defined as follows defines a probability measure on
\Omega
:

\mathbb{P}{\left(A_{n}\times\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{n}}\right)}:=\mathbb{P}_{n}{\left(A_{n}\right)}\,.

Proof.

We consider the ring

\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{A\times\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{n}}\,:\;A\subset\Omega_{n}\text{ is measurable}\right\}

and make the observation that
\mathbb{P}
is additive and positive on
\mathcal{R}

and
\mathbb{P}(\emptyset)=0
. Next, let
\left(A_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be
an increasing sequence of sets in
\mathcal{R}
such that
A:=\bigcup_{j}A_{j}\in\mathcal{R}
.
Then, there exists
\tilde{A}_{1}\subset\Omega_{0}^{n}
such that

A_{1}=\tilde{A}_{1}\times\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{n}}
and since
A_{1}\subset A_{2}\subset\dots\subset A
,
for every
j>1
, we conclude
A_{j}=\tilde{A_{j}}\times\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{n}}

for some
\tilde{A}_{j}\subset\Omega_{n}
. Therefore,
\mathbb{P}(A_{j})=\mathbb{P}_{n}(\tilde{A}_{j})\to\mathbb{P}_{n}(\tilde{A})=\mathbb{P}(A)

where
A=\tilde{A}\times\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{n}}
. We have thus proved
that
\mathbb{P}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,1]
can be extended to a measure on the Borel-
\sigma
-Algebra
on
\Omega
(See
[
2
, Theorem 6-2]
).
∎

We define for
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
the mapping

\hat{\tau}_{z}:\,\hat{\Omega}\to\hat{\Omega}\,,\qquad\hat{\omega}\mapsto\hat{\tau}_{z}\hat{\omega}\,,\quad\mbox{where }\left(\hat{\tau}_{z}\hat{\omega}\right)_{\xi_{i}}=\hat{\omega}_{\xi_{i}+z}\mbox{ component wise}\,.

Remark 2.58
.

In this paper, we consider particularly

\Omega_{0}=\left\{0,1\right\}
. Then
\hat{\Omega}:=\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

is equivalent to the power set of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and every
\hat{\omega}\in\hat{\Omega}

is a sequence of
0
and
1
corresponding to a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Shifting the set
\hat{\omega}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
corresponds
to an application of
\hat{\tau}_{z}
to
\hat{\omega}\in\hat{\Omega}
.

Now, let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)
be a stationary ergodic random open set and
let
r>0
. Recalling (
2.1
) the map
\omega\mapsto\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{-r}(\omega)

is measurable due to Lemma
2.41
and we can define

\mathbb{X}_{r}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}{\left(\omega\right)}\right)}:=2r\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{-\frac{r}{2}}{\left(\omega\right)}
.

Lemma 2.59
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is a stationary ergodic random
open set then the set

\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{X}_{r}(\omega):=\mathbb{X}_{r}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}{\left(\omega\right)}\right)}:=2r\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{-r}{\left(\omega\right)}

(2.51)

is a stationary random point process w.r.t.
2r\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Proof.

By a simple scaling we can w.l.o.g. assume
2r=1
and write
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{X}_{r}
.
Evidently,
\mathbb{X}
corresponds to a process on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with values in

\Omega_{0}=\left\{0,1\right\}
writing
\mathbb{X}(z)=1
if
z\in\mathbb{X}

and
\mathbb{X}(z)=0
if
z\not\in\mathbb{X}
. In particular, we write
\left(\omega,z\right)\mapsto\mathbb{X}{\left(\omega,z\right)}
.
This process is stationary as the shift invariance of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
induces
a shift-invariance of
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
with respect to
\hat{\tau}_{z}
.
It remains to observe that the probabilities
\mathbb{P}{\left(\mathbb{X}(z)=1\right)}
and

\mathbb{P}{\left(\mathbb{X}(z)=0\right)}
induce a random measure on
\hat{\Omega}
in the
way described in Remark
2.58
.
∎

Remark 2.60
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is mixing one can follow the lines of the proof of Lemma

2.34
to find that
\mathbb{X}_{r}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}{\left(\omega\right)}\right)}

is ergodic. However, in the general case
\mathbb{X}_{r}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}{\left(\omega\right)}\right)}

is not ergodic. This is due to the fact that by nature
\left(\tau_{z}\right)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

on
\Omega
has more invariant sets than
\left(\tau_{x}\right)_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.
For sufficiently complex geometries the map
\Omega\to\hat{\Omega}

is onto.

Definition 2.61

(Jointly stationary)
.

We
call a point process
\mathbb{X}
with values in
2r\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to be strongly
jointly stationary with a random set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
if the functions
\chi_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)}
,

\chi_{\mathbb{X}(\omega)}
are jointly stationary w.r.t. the dynamical system

\left(\tau_{2rx}\right)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
on
\Omega
.

3
Quantifying Nonlocal Regularity Properties
of the Geometry

3.1
Microscopic Regularity

Figure 2:
How to fit a ball into a cone.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a Lipschitz domain. Then
for every
p_{0}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
with
\delta(p_{0})>0
the following
holds: For every
\delta<\delta\left(p_{0}\right)
and
M:=M_{\delta}(p_{0})>0

there exists
y\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
with
\left|p_{0}-y\right|=\frac{\delta}{4}

such that with
r\left(p_{0}\right):=\frac{\delta}{4\left(1+M\right)}

it holds
\mathbb{B}_{r\left(p_{0}\right)}{\left(y\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{\delta/2}{\left(p_{0}\right)}
.

Proof.

We can assume that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is locally a cone as in Figure
2
.
With regard to Figure
2
, for
p_{0}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

with
\delta
and
M
as in the statement we can place a right circular
cone with vertex (apex)
p_{0}
and axis
\nu
and an aperture
\theta=\pi-2\arctan M

inside
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}{\left(p_{0}\right)}
, where
\alpha=\arctan M
. In other
words, it holds
\tan\left(\alpha\right)=\tan\left(\frac{\pi-\theta}{2}\right)=M
.
Along the axis we may select
y
with
\left|p_{0}-y\right|=\frac{\delta}{4}
.
Then the distance
R
of
y
to the cone is given through

\left|y-p_{0}\right|^{2}=R^{2}+R^{2}\tan^{2}\left(\frac{\pi-\theta}{2}\right)\quad\Rightarrow\quad R=\frac{\left|y-p_{0}\right|}{\sqrt{1+M^{2}}}\,.

In particular
r\left(p_{0}\right)
as defined above satisfies the
claim.
∎

Continuity properties of
\delta
,
M
and
\varrho

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a Lipschitz
domain and recall (
1.7
). Then
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is

\delta_{\Delta}
-regular in the sense of Definition
2.22
.
In particular,
\delta_{\Delta}:\,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\to\mathbb{R}
is locally Lipschitz
continuous with Lipschitz constant
4
and for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)

and
\tilde{p}\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon\delta}{\left(p\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
it holds

\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}>\delta_{\Delta}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}>\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}-\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|>\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}\,.

(3.1)

Remark 3.3
.

The latter lemma does
not

imply global Lipschitz regularity of
\delta_{\Delta}
. It could
be that
2\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}<\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<3\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}

and
p
and
\tilde{p}
are connected by a path inside
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

with the shortest path of length
10\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}
. Then Lemma

3.2
would have to be applied successively
along this path yielding an estimate of
\left|\delta_{\Delta}{\left(p\right)}-\delta_{\Delta}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\right|\leq 40\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

It is straight forward to verify that
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\varepsilon\delta_{\Delta}(p)

implies
\delta_{\Delta}(\tilde{p})>(1-\varepsilon)\delta_{\Delta}(p)
and
we conclude with Lemma
2.24
.
∎

With regard to Lemma
2.3
, the relevant
quantity for local extension operators is related to
\delta{\left(p\right)}/\sqrt{4M{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}
,
where
M(p)
is the related Lipschitz constant. While we can quantify

\delta{\left(p\right)}
in terms of
\delta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}
and
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|
,
this does not work for
M{\left(p\right)}
. Hence we cannot quantify
\delta{\left(p\right)}/\sqrt{4M{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}

in terms of its neighbors. This drawback is compensated by a variational
trick in the following statement.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a Lipschitz domain and let
\delta\leq\delta_{\Delta}

satisfy (
3.1
) such that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

is
\delta
-regular. For
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and let
M_{r}{\left(p\right)}

be given in (
1.8
) and define for
n,K\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r<\delta{\left(p\right)}}r\sqrt{4M_{r}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}^{-n}\,,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{n,K}(p)

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\delta\leq\delta(p)\,:\;\sup_{r<2^{-K}\delta}r\sqrt{4M_{r}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}^{-n}\geq 2^{-K}\rho_{n}(p)\right\}\,.

(3.3)

Then for fixed
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
the functions
r\mapsto M_{r}(p)

is right continuous and monotone increasing (i.e. u.s.c.). Furthermore,

\rho_{n}
is positive and locally Lipschitz continuous on
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

with Lipschitz constant
4
and
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is
\rho
-regular
in the sense of Definition
2.22
. In particular,
for
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\varepsilon\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}
it holds

\displaystyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}>\rho_{n}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}>\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}-\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|>\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\rho_{n}{\left(p\right)}\,.

(3.4)

Furthermore,
\hat{\rho}_{n,K}\leq\delta
is well defined.

Remark 3.5
.

Like in Remark
3.3
this does
not

imply global Lipschitz regularity of
\rho_{n}
or
\hat{\rho}_{n}
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Every Lipschitz domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

has extension order
1
and symmetric extension order
2
.

Proof.

This follows from
\hat{\rho}_{n,3}\leq\delta
and Lemmas
2.3

and
2.7
applied to
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\hat{\rho}_{n,3}}{\left(p_{0}\right)}

and
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\rho_{n}}{\left(p_{0}\right)}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

Right continuity of
r\mapsto M_{r}(p)
follows because for every

0<r<R
because
M
being Lipschitz constant of
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
in

\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(p\right)}
implies
M
being Lipschitz constant of
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

in
\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(p\right)}
.

Let
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\varepsilon\rho{\left(p\right)}<\varepsilon\delta(p)

implying
\delta{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\geq\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\delta{\left(p\right)}

by Lemma
3.2
. For every
\eta>0

let
r_{\eta}\in\left(\rho(p),\delta{\left(p\right)}\right)
such that
\rho{\left(p\right)}\leq\left(1+\eta\right)r_{\eta}\sqrt{4M_{r_{\eta}}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}^{-n}
.
Since
r_{\eta}>\rho{\left(p\right)}
and
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\varepsilon\rho{\left(p\right)}

we find
\mathbb{B}_{r_{\eta}}{\left(p\right)}\supset\mathbb{B}_{\left(1-\varepsilon\right)r_{\eta}}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}

and hence
M_{\left(1-\varepsilon\right)r_{\eta}}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\leq M_{r_{\eta}}{\left(p\right)}
.
This implies at the same time that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is
\rho
-regular
and that

\rho{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\geq\frac{\left(1-\varepsilon\right)r_{\eta}}{\sqrt{4M_{\left(1-\varepsilon\right)r_{\eta}}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}^{2}+2}^{n}}\geq\frac{\left(1-\varepsilon\right)r_{\eta}}{\sqrt{4M_{r_{\eta}}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}^{n}}\geq\frac{\left(1-\varepsilon\right)}{\left(1+\eta\right)}\rho{\left(p\right)}\,.

Since
\eta
was arbitrary, we conclude
\rho{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\geq\left(1-\varepsilon\right)\rho{\left(p\right)}
.
Moreover, we find
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\rho{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}
.
And we conclude the first part with Lemma
2.24
.

Second, it holds for every
r<\delta
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
that

\varepsilon r\sqrt{4M_{r}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}^{-n}\leq\varepsilon r\sqrt{4M_{\varepsilon r}{\left(p\right)}^{2}+2}^{-n}

and choosing
\varepsilon=2^{-K}
and taking the supremum on both sides,
we infer
\hat{\rho}_{n,K}\leq\delta
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
a locally
\left(\delta,M\right)
-regular open set, where we restrict

\delta
by
\delta\left(\cdot\right)\leq\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{4}
. Then there
exists a countable number of points
\left(p_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

such that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is completely covered by balls
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}\left(p_{k}\right)}{\left(p_{k}\right)}

where
\tilde{\rho}\left(p\right):=2^{-5}\rho_{n}\left(p\right)

for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Writing

\tilde{\rho}_{k}:=\tilde{\rho}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\,,\qquad\delta_{k}:=\delta{\left(p_{k}\right)}\,.

For two such balls with
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{k}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{i}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}\neq\emptyset

it holds

\displaystyle\frac{15}{16}\tilde{\rho}_{i}\leq\tilde{\rho}_{k}\leq\frac{16}{15}\tilde{\rho}_{i}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{31}{15}\min\left\{\tilde{\rho}_{i},\tilde{\rho}_{k}\right\}\geq\left|p_{i}-p_{k}\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\max\left\{\tilde{\rho}_{i},\tilde{\rho}_{k}\right\}\,.

Furthermore, there exists
\mathfrak{r}_{k}\geq\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{k}}{32\left(1+M_{\tilde{\rho}(p_{k})}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\right)}

and
y_{k}
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{k}}{\left(y_{k}\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{k}/8}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

and
\mathbb{B}_{2\mathfrak{r}_{k}}{\left(y_{k}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{2\mathfrak{r}_{j}}{\left(y_{j}\right)}=\emptyset

for
k\neq j
.

Proof.

The existence of the points and Balls satisfying (
3.5
)
follows from Theorem
2.25
, in particular
(
2.35
). It holds for
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{k}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{i}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}\neq\emptyset

\left|p_{i}-p_{k}\right|\leq\tilde{\rho_{i}}+\tilde{\rho}_{k}\leq\left(\frac{16}{15}+1\right)\tilde{\rho}_{i}\,.

Lemma
3.1
yields existence of
y_{k}
such
that
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{k}}{\left(y_{k}\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{k}/8}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
.
The latter implies
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{k}}{\left(y_{k}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{j}}{\left(y_{j}\right)}=\emptyset

for
k\neq j
.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a locally
\left(\delta,M\right)
-regular
open set and let
M_{0}\in(0,+\infty]
such that for every
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

there exists
\delta>0
,
M<M_{0}
such that
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is
(\delta,M)
-regular
in
p
. For
\alpha\in(0,1]
let
\eta(p)=\alpha\delta_{\Delta}(p)

from Lemma
3.2
or
\eta(p)=\alpha\rho_{n}(p)

from Lemma
3.4
and define

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(p\right)}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\delta>\eta{\left(p\right)}}\inf_{M}\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\text{ is }\left(\delta,M\right)\text{-regular in }p\right\}\,.

(3.6)

Then, for fixed
\xi
,
\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(\cdot\right)}:\;\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\to\mathbb{R}

is upper semicontinuous and on each bounded measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

the quantity

\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A):=\sup_{p\in\overline{A}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}M_{[\eta]}{\left(p\right)}

(3.7)

with

\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A)=0
if
\overline{A}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}=\emptyset

is well defined. The functions

\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A,\cdot):\;\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\,,\quad\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(A,x\right)}:=\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A+x)\quad\text{with }\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A,0)=\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A)

are upper semicontinuous.

Remark 3.9
.

Note at this point that
M_{[\eta,r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}

defined in (
1.11
)
is a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and different from
M_{[\eta]}
.

Notation 3.10
.

The infimum in (
3.6
) is a
\liminf
for
\delta\searrow\eta(p)
.
We sometimes use the special notation …………………..???????????????????????……………..

M_{[\eta],\mathfrak{r}}(x):=M_{[\eta],\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(0\right)}}(x)\,.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

Let
p,\tilde{p}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
with
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|<\varepsilon\eta(p)
.
Writing
\tilde{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}
and
r\left(p,\varepsilon\right):=\left(\frac{1}{1-2\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\right)\eta{\left(p\right)}

and

M{\left(p,\varepsilon\right)}:=\inf_{M}\left\{\mathbb{B}_{r\left(p,\varepsilon\right)}{\left(p\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\text{ is }M\text{-Lipschitz graph}\right\}

as well as we observe from
\eta
-regularity that
\mathbb{B}_{\eta\left(\tilde{p}\right)}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{r\left(p,\varepsilon\right)}{\left(p\right)}

and
\mathbb{B}_{\eta\left(p\right)}{\left(p\right)}\subset\mathbb{B}_{r\left(\tilde{p},\tilde{\varepsilon}\right)}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}
.
Hence we find

\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\leq M{\left(p,\varepsilon\right)}\,.

Observing that
M{\left(p,\varepsilon\right)}\searrow\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(p\right)}
as
\varepsilon\to 0

we find
\limsup_{\tilde{p}\to p}\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}\leq\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(p\right)}

and
\mathrm{M}
is u.s.c.

Let
x\to 0
. First observe that
\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}(A)=\max_{y\in\overline{A}}\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(y\right)}
.
The set
\overline{A}
is compact and hence
\overline{A}+x\to\overline{A}

in the Hausdorff metric as
x\to 0
. Let
y_{x}\in\overline{A}+x

such that
\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(y_{x}\right)}=\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}\left(A,x\right)
.
Since
\overline{A}+x\to\overline{A}
w.l.o.g. we find
y_{x}\to y

converges and
y\in\overline{A}
. Hence

\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(y\right)}\geq\limsup_{x\to 0}\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(y_{x}\right)}=\limsup_{x\to 0}\mathrm{M}_{[\eta]}{\left(A,x\right)}\,.

In particular,
M_{[\eta],A}{\left(\cdot\right)}
is u.s.c. The u.s.c of
\mathfrak{m}_{[\eta]}{\left(p,\xi\right)}

can be proved similarly.
∎

Measurability and Integrability of Extended Variables

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>0
, let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

be a Lipschitz domain and let
\eta,r:\,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous
such that
\eta\leq\mathfrak{r}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is
\eta
- and
r
-regular.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
let
\eta(p)=\varepsilon\delta(p)
from Lemma
3.2

or
\eta(p)=\varepsilon\rho_{n}(p)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, from Lemma
3.4
.
Then
\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
from (
1.10
)
is measurable and
M_{[\eta,r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
from (
1.11
)
is upper semicontinuous.

In what follows, we write
A_{\eta,\mathfrak{r}}:=F^{-1}{\left((0,\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{r})\right)}

for

F:=\inf_{p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}f_{p}\,,\qquad f_{p}{\left(x\right)}:=\begin{cases}\eta{\left(p\right)}&\text{if }x\in\mathbb{B}_{r(p)}{\left(p\right)}\\
2\mathfrak{r}&\text{else}\end{cases}\,.

Proof.

Step 1: We write
A=A_{\eta,\mathfrak{r}}
for simplicity. Let
\left(p_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

be a dense subset. If
x\in\mathbb{B}_{r{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}
for some
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

then also
x\in\mathbb{B}_{r{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}
for
\left|p-\tilde{p}\right|

sufficiently small, by continuity of
\eta
. Hence every
f_{p}

is upper semicontinuous and it holds
F=\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{p_{i}}
.
In particular,
F
is measurable and so is the set
A
. This implies

\eta_{[r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}=\chi_{A}F
is measurable.

Step 2: We show that for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
the preimage
M_{[\eta,r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{-1}{\left([a,+\infty)\right)}

is closed. Let
\left(x_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence with
M_{[\eta,r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\left(x_{k}\right)}\in[a,+\infty)
.
Let
\left(p_{k}\right)\subset\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
be a sequence with
\left|x_{k}-p_{k}\right|\leq r{\left(p_{k}\right)}
.
W.l.o.g. assume
p_{k}\to p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and
x_{k}\to x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Since
r
is continuous, it follows
\left|x-p\right|\leq r{\left(p\right)}
.
On the other hand
M_{[\eta]}(p)\geq\limsup_{k\to\infty}M_{[\eta]}(p_{k})

and thus
M_{[\eta,r],\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\left(x\right)}\geq M_{[\eta,r]}(p)\geq a
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Under the assumptions
of Lemma
3.11
let
\tilde{\eta}:=\eta_{[\frac{\eta}{8}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
only depending on the dimension

d
such that for every bounded open domain
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
and
k\in[0,4)

it holds

\displaystyle\int_{A_{\eta,\mathfrak{r}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\chi_{\tilde{\eta}>0}\tilde{\eta}^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{4}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\eta^{1-\alpha}M_{[\frac{\eta}{4}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{d-2}\,,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\int_{A_{\eta,\mathfrak{r}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\tilde{\eta}^{-\alpha}M_{[k\frac{\eta}{8},\frac{\eta}{8}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{r}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{4}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\eta^{1-\alpha}M_{[k\frac{\eta}{8},\frac{\eta}{4}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{r}M_{[\frac{\eta}{4}],\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{d-2}\,.

(3.9)

Finally, it holds

x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\eta{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\eta{\left(p\right)}>\tilde{\eta}{\left(x\right)}>\frac{3}{4}\eta{\left(p\right)}\,.

(3.10)

Remark 3.13
.

Estimates (
3.8
)–(
3.9
)
are only rough estimates and better results could be obtained via
more sophisticated calculations that make use of particular features
of given geometries.

Proof.

We write
A=A_{\eta,\mathfrak{r}}
for simplicity. Step 1: Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

with
\tilde{\eta}(x)>0
let

p_{x}\in\mathrm{argmin}\left\{\eta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\,:\;\tilde{x}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\,\text{s.t. }x\in\overline{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\eta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}\right\}\,.

(3.11)

Such
p_{x}
exists because
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is locally compact. We
observe with help of the definition of
p_{x}
, the triangle inequality
and (
2.34
)

x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\eta{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}\leq\eta{\left(p\right)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left|p-p_{x}\right|<\frac{\eta{\left(p\right)}}{4}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}>\frac{3}{4}\eta{\left(p\right)}\,.

The last line particularly implies (
3.10
)
and

\forall p\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\;\forall x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\eta{\left(p\right)}}{8}}{\left(p\right)}:\quad\tilde{\eta}{\left(x\right)}>\frac{3\eta{\left(p\right)}}{4}\,.

Step 2: By Theorem
2.25
we can chose a
countable number of points
\left(p_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

such that
\Gamma=\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is completely covered by balls
B_{k}:=\mathbb{B}_{\xi{\left(p_{k}\right)}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}

where
\xi{\left(p\right)}:=2^{-4}\eta{\left(p\right)}
. For simplicity of notation we
write
\eta_{k}:=\eta{\left(p_{k}\right)}
and
\xi_{k}:=\xi{\left(p_{k}\right)}
. Assume

x\in A
with
p_{x}\in\Gamma
given by (
3.11
).
Since the balls
B_{k}
cover
\Gamma
, there exists
p_{k}
with

\left|p_{x}-p_{k}\right|<\xi_{k}=2^{-4}\eta_{k}
, implying
\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}<\frac{2^{4}}{2^{4}-1}\eta_{k}

and hence
\left|x-p_{k}\right|\leq\left(2^{-4}+\frac{2^{-3}2^{4}}{2^{4}-1}\right)\eta_{k}<\frac{3}{16}\eta_{k}
.
Hence we find

\forall x\in A\,\;\exists p_{k}\;:\quad x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{3}{16}\eta_{k}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}\,.

Step 3: For
p\in\Gamma
with
x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}\eta{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}}{\left(p_{x}\right)}

we can distinguish two cases:

1.

\eta{\left(p\right)}\geq\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}
: Then
p_{x}\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{3}{8}\eta{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}

and hence
\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}\geq\frac{5}{8}\eta{\left(p\right)}
by (
2.34
).

2.

\eta{\left(p\right)}<\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}
: Then
p\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{3}{8}\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}}{\left(p_{x}\right)}

and hence
\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}>\frac{1-\frac{3}{8}}{1-\frac{6}{8}}\eta{\left(p\right)}=\frac{5}{2}\eta{\left(p\right)}

by (
2.34
).

and hence

x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}\eta{\left(p\right)}}{\left(p\right)}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\tilde{\eta}{\left(x\right)}=\eta{\left(p_{x}\right)}>\frac{5}{8}\eta{\left(p\right)}\,.

Step 4: Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed and define
B_{k}=\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{4}\eta_{k}}{\left(p_{k}\right)}
,

M_{k}:=M_{\frac{1}{4}\eta_{k}}(p_{k})
. By construction, every
B_{j}

with
B_{j}\cap B_{k}\neq\emptyset
satisfies
\eta_{j}\geq\frac{1}{2}\eta_{k}

and hence if
B_{j}\cap B_{k}\neq\emptyset
and
B_{i}\cap B_{j}\neq\emptyset

we find
\left|p_{j}-p_{i}\right|\geq\frac{1}{4}\eta_{k}
and
\left|p_{j}-p_{k}\right|\leq 3\eta_{k}
.
This implies that

\exists C>0:\;\forall k\quad\#\left\{j\,:\;B_{j}\cap B_{k}\neq\emptyset\right\}\leq C\,.

We further observe that the minimal surface of
B_{k}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

is given in case when
B_{k}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is a cone with opening
angle
\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan M{\left(p_{k}\right)}
. The surface area of
B_{k}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

in this case is bounded by
\frac{1}{d-1}\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\right|\eta_{k}^{d-1}\left(M_{k}+1\right)^{2-d}
.
This particularly implies up to a constant independent from
k
:

\displaystyle\int_{A\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\tilde{\eta}^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k:\,B_{k}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset}\int_{A\cap B_{k}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\eta_{k}^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k:\,B_{k}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset}\int_{A\cap B_{k}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\eta^{1-\alpha}M_{[\frac{\eta}{4}]}^{d-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{A\cap\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{4}}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\eta^{1-\alpha}M_{[\frac{\eta}{4}]}^{d-2}\,.

The second integral formula follows in a similar way.
∎

3.2
Mesoscopic Regularity and Isotropic
Cone Mixing

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)
be a stationary
and ergodic random open set such that

\mathbb{P}{\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\mathbb{I}=\emptyset\right)}<1\,.

Then there exists
\mathfrak{r}>0
and a positive, monotonically decreasing
function
\tilde{f}
such that almost surely
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)
is
(\mathfrak{r},\tilde{f})
-mesoscopic
regular.

Proof.

Step 1: For some
\mathfrak{r}>0
and with positive probability
p_{\mathfrak{r}}>0

the set
(0,1)^{d}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
contains a ball with radius
5\sqrt{d}\mathfrak{r}
.
Otherwise, for every
r>0
the set
(0,1)^{d}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
almost surely
does not contain an open ball with radius
r
. In particular with
probability
1
the set
(0,1)^{d}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
does not contain any
ball. Hence
(0,1)^{d}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}=\emptyset
almost surely, contradicting
the assumptions.

Step 2: We define
