\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}C\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{b}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})\\
\mathbb{B}_{2d_{b}}{\left(x_{b}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\,.

(6.5)

Hence, we encounter the conditions
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset

and
\mathbb{B}_{2d_{b}}{\left(x_{b}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}\neq\emptyset
as well
as

\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|\leq 3\max\left\{d_{a},d_{b}\right\}\,.

In particular, we conclude the symmetric condition

\mathbb{B}_{4d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset\,,\quad\mathbb{B}_{4d_{a}}{\left(x_{b}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset\,,\qquad\mathbb{B}_{2d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\cap\mathbb{B}_{2d_{b}}{\left(x_{b}\right)}\neq\emptyset

and

\text{R.H.S of }\eqref{eq:thm:Delaunay-final-help-2}\leq\mathrm{I}_{0}\,.

(6.6)

Similarly

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\sum_{a:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{a}>0}\sum_{b:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{b}<0}

\displaystyle\frac{d_{b}^{-\alpha\frac{s}{r}}\left|\partial_{l}\Phi_{b}\right|}{D_{l+}^{\Phi}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}u\right|^{s}\leq I_{\alpha}\,.

(6.7)

Step 3:
We now derive an estimate for
I_{\alpha}
. For pairs

(a,b)
with
d_{b}\leq d_{a},\,\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|\leq 3d_{a}

let
y_{a,b}:=\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{n(a,b)}\right)
be a discrete
path on the Delaunay grid of
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}
with length smaller than

2\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|
(this exists due to
[
28
]
)
that connects
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
. By the minimal mutual distance
of points, this particularly implies that
n(a,b)\leq 6d_{a}/2\mathfrak{r}

and the path lies completely within
\mathbb{B}_{4.5\,d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}
. Because

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq n(a,b)^{s}\sum_{k=1}^{n(a,b)-1}\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{k+1}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq 6d_{a}^{s}/2\mathfrak{r}\sum_{k=1}^{n(a,b)-1}\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{k+1}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

it holds with (
6.3
)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)(x)\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{a}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{6d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left(\sum_{e\sim f}F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}{\left(\delta(e,f)\right)}\left(\left|x-x_{e}\right|^{s}+\left|x-x_{f}\right|^{s}\right)\right.\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\left.\left|x_{e}-x_{f}\right|^{2s}\left(\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{e}\right)}}+\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{f}\right)}}+d_{a}^{s-1}\chi_{P_{\delta,e,f}}\right)\right)\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{s}

We make use of
\left|x-x_{e}\right|^{s}\leq 2^{s}\left(\left|x-x_{a}\right|^{s}+\left|x_{a}-x_{e}\right|^{s}\right)\leq 2^{s}\left(\left|x-x_{a}\right|^{s}+d_{a}^{s}\right)

and
\left|x_{e}-x_{f}\right|^{2s}\leq Cd_{a}^{2s}
and
B_{e,f}:=\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(\{x_{e},x_{f}\}\right)}\cup P_{\delta,e,f}

to find

\left|\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)(x)\right|^{s}\leq Cd_{a}^{4s}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{6d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left(\sum_{e\sim f}F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}{\left(\delta(e,f)\right)}\left(\left|x-x_{a}\right|^{s}+d_{a}^{s}\right)\,\chi_{B_{e,f}}\right)\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{s}\,.

In the integrals
I_{\alpha}
, any of the integrals
\int\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

has
\left|x-x_{a}\right|<2d_{a}
and we can use an estimate of the
form

\left|\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)(x)\right|^{s}\leq Cd_{a}^{5s}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{6d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left(\sum_{e\sim f}F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}{\left(\delta(e,f)\right)}\chi_{B_{e,f}}\right)\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{s}\,.

With this estimate, and using

\#\left\{b:\,\mathbb{B}_{4d_{b}}{\left(x_{b}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset,\,d_{b}\leq d_{a},\,\left|x_{a}-x_{b}\right|\leq 3d_{a}\right\}\leq Cd_{a}^{d}

the integral
I_{\alpha}
can be controlled through

I_{\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\sum_{a:\,\mathbb{B}_{4d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset}d_{a}^{2d+5s-\alpha\frac{s}{r}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{6d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left(\sum_{e\sim f}F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}{\left(\delta(e,f)\right)}\chi_{B_{e,f}}\right)\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{s}\,.

Denoting

\displaystyle f(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a}d_{a}^{2d+5s-\alpha\frac{s}{r}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{6d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\,,

\displaystyle f(\omega,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a:\,\mathbb{B}_{4d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\neq\emptyset}d_{a}^{2d+5s-\alpha\frac{s}{r}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{6d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\,,

\displaystyle g(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{e\sim f}F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}{\left(\delta(e,f)\right)}\chi_{B_{e,f}}\,,

and using
u\equiv 0
outside
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
, we observe

I_{\alpha}\leq\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}f(\omega,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\,g(\omega)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)^{\frac{p-s}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{s}{p}}\,.

Step 4:
Since every quantity related to the distribution
of
d_{a}
is distributed exponentially, we can be very generous
with this variable. We observe

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{a}f_{a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\chi_{A_{1,i}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

but for every fixed
x
(and using that
x\in\mathbb{B}_{2d_{a_{i}}}{\left(x_{a_{i}}\right)}
)
using again Jensens inequality

\int_{A_{1,i}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)\right|^{s}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}{\left(y_{i}\right)}}\left(\left|\nabla\left(u-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right|^{s}d_{a_{i}}^{s}+\left|u-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\right)\,.

Having this in mind, we may sum over all
y_{i}
to find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}

\displaystyle\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{a}f_{a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}C\sum_{a}\chi_{2d_{a}}\sum_{b\sim a}d_{a}^{s}\,\chi_{P(x_{a},x_{b},\delta(a,b))}\left(\left|\nabla\left(u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right)\right|^{s}+\left|u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\right)\,.

With the splitting
u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u=u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta(a,b)}u+\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s},\delta(a,b)}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u

and Lemmas
2.18
and
2.17

it follows with
F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}
from (
6.4
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}

\displaystyle\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{a}f_{a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a_{i}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}C\sum_{a}\sum_{b\sim a}F_{s}^{\mathfrak{s},1}{\left(\delta(a,b)\right)}\left(\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\cup\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{b}\right)})}^{s}+\left(2d_{a}\right)^{s}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{s}(P(x_{a},x_{b},\delta(a,b)))}^{s}\right)d_{a}^{d+s}

by a restructuration, the right hand side is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}C\left(\sum_{a}\chi_{2d_{a}}d_{a}^{3s+d}\right)

\displaystyle g(\omega)\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}f_{1}(\omega)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}Bg(\omega)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)^{\frac{p-s}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{s}{p}}\,,

where

\displaystyle f_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a}\chi_{2d_{a}}\left(2d_{a}\right)^{s+d}

Step 4: We can replace in the above calculations
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
by
n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
.
By Lemma
6.6
we can extend
f(\omega,n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

to
f(\omega)|_{Rn\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}
for some fixed
R>1
and on
R\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
we can
use standard ergodic theory. Hence, the expressions in
\delta
and

d_{a}
converge to a constant as
n\to\infty
provided

\mathbb{E}{\left(\left(f_{1}g\right)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}+\left(fg\right)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)}<\infty\,.

(6.8)

However,
f
,
f_{1}
and
g
are stationary by definition and

f
and
g
or
f_{1}
and
g
are independent. Since
f
and

f_{1}
clearly have finite expectation by the exponential distribution
of
d_{a}
and Lemma
3.18
, we only mention that
due to the strong mixing of
\delta
and its independence from the
distribution of connections

\mathbb{E}(g^{\frac{p}{p-s}})\leq\mathbb{E}{\left(\sum_{e\sim f}\chi_{B_{e,f}}\right)}\,\mathbb{E}{\left({\left(\delta^{-s-d}+\delta^{1+s-2d}\right)}^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)}

and thus (
6.8
) holds.
∎

The work
[
28
]
which we used in the last proof also
opens the door to demonstrate the following result which will be used
in part III of this series to prove regularity properties of the homogenized
equation.

Theorem 6.7
.

For fixed
y_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}
and every

\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}
let
P(y_{0},\tilde{y})=\left(y_{0},y_{1}(\tilde{y}),\dots,y_{N}(\tilde{y})\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

with
y_{N}(\tilde{y})=\tilde{y}
be the shortest path of points
in
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}
connecting
y_{0}
and
\tilde{y}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and
having length
L(y_{0},\tilde{y})
. Then there exists

\displaystyle\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}:\,[0,L(y_{0},\tilde{y})]\times\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle(t,z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}(t,z)

such that
\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}(t,\cdot)
is invertible for every

t
and
\left\|\partial_{t}\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2
.
For
R>1
let

N_{y_{0},R}(x):=\#\left\{\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(y_{0}\right)}\cap\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}:\,\exists t:\,x\in\gamma_{y_{0},\tilde{y}}{\left(t,\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(0\right)}\right)}\right\}\,.

Then there exists
C>0
such that for every
y_{0}
it holds

N_{y_{0},R}(x)\leq C\left(R^{d}-\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{d}\right)\quad\text{for }\left|x-y_{0}\right|<2R\,,\qquad N_{y_{0},R}(x)=0\quad\text{else.}

Proof.

The function
\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}
consists basically of pipes
connecting
y_{i}(\tilde{y})
with
y_{i+1}(\tilde{y})
that conically
become smaller within the ball
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{2}}{\left(y_{i}(\tilde{y})\right)}

before entering the pipe and vice versa in
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{2}}{\left(y_{i+1}(\tilde{y})\right)}
.
Defining

N_{y_{0},r,R}(x):=\#\left\{\tilde{y}\in{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(y_{0}\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(y_{0}\right)}\right)}\cap\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}:\,\exists t:\,x\in\gamma_{y_{0},\tilde{y}}{\left(t,\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(0\right)}\right)}\right\}

[
28
]
implies
N_{y_{0},r,R}(x)=0
for all
\left|x-y_{0}\right|>2R

but also due to the minimal mutual distance
N_{y_{0},r,R}(x)\leq CR^{d-1}(R-r)
,
where
C
depends only on
\mathfrak{r}
and
d
. Hence writing
\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor:=\min\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\,n+1>x\right\}

we can estimate for every
K\in\mathbb{N}

N_{y_{0},K}(x)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}N_{y_{0},k,k+1}(x)\leq C\sum_{k=\left\lfloor\frac{x}{2}\right\rfloor}^{K-1}\left(k+1\right)^{d-1}\leq C\left(K^{d}-\left\lfloor\frac{x}{2}\right\rfloor^{d}\right)\,.

∎

We close this section by proving Theorem
1.16
.

Proof of Theorem
1.16
.

The statement on the support is provided by Theorem
4.10

and the fact that we restrict to functions with support in
m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
.
Hence in the following we can apply all cited results to
\mathbb{B}_{m^{1-\beta}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}

instead of
m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
. According to Lemmas
4.7

and
5.2
–
5.3
and to Theorem
6.5

we need only need to ensure

\mathbb{E}{\left(\delta^{-s-d}+\delta^{1+s-2d}\right)}^{\frac{p}{p-s}}+\mathbb{E}\left(1+M_{[\frac{1}{8}\delta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{r}+\mathbb{E}\left|\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right|^{-\frac{sr}{s-r}}<\infty\,,

since
d_{a}
is distributed exponentially and the corresponding
terms are bounded as long as
r\neq s\neq p
. We note that the exponential
distribution of
M
allows us to restrict to the study of
\delta

and
\tilde{\rho}
.

According to Lemma
6.4
it is sufficient
that
\max\left\{\frac{p\left(s+d\right)}{p-s},\frac{p(2d-s-1)}{p-s}\right\}\leq\beta

and
\frac{sr}{s-r}\leq\beta+d-1
.
∎

6.2
Boolean Model for the Poisson Ball
Process

The following argumentation will be strongly based on the so called
void probability. This is the probability
\mathbb{P}_{0}(A)
to not find
any point of the point process in a given open set
A
and is given
by (
2.49
) i.e.
\mathbb{P}_{0}(A):=e^{-\lambda\left|A\right|}
.
The void probability for the ball process is given accordingly by

\mathbb{P}_{0}(A):=e^{-\lambda\left|\overline{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(A\right)}}\right|}\,,\qquad\overline{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(A\right)}}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\mathrm{dist}{\left(x,A\right)}\leq 1\right\}\,,

which is the probability that no ball intersects with
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\left(\omega\right):=\bigcup_{i}B_{i}(\omega)

(or
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\left(\omega\right):=\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\bigcup_{i}B_{i}(\omega)
)
and define

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{\delta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\,:\;\tilde{x}\in\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\,\text{s.t. }x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\right\}\,,

where
\min\emptyset:=0
for convenience. Then
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
is
almost surely locally
\left(\delta,M\right)
regular and for every

\gamma<1
,
\beta<d+2
it holds

\mathbb{E}{\left(\delta^{-\gamma}\right)}+\mathbb{E}{\left(\tilde{\delta}^{-\gamma-1}\right)}+\mathbb{E}{\left(\tilde{M}_{[0]}^{\beta}\right)}<\infty\,.

Furthermore, it holds
\hat{d}\leq d-1
and
\alpha=0
in inequalities
(
4.9
) and (
4.4
).
Furthermore the extension order and symmetric extension order are
both
n=0
. If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\left(\omega\right):=\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\overline{\bigcup_{i}B_{i}}(\omega)

the above holds with
\alpha
replaced by
1
and with extension
order
n=1
and symmetric extension order
n=2
.

Remark 6.9
.

We observe that the union of balls has better properties than the
complement.

Proof.

We study only
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\left(\omega\right):=\bigcup_{i}B_{i}(\omega)

since
\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\overline{\bigcup_{i}B_{i}}(\omega)
is the complement
sharing the same boundary. Hence, in case
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}(\omega)=\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\overline{\bigcup_{i}B_{i}}(\omega)
,
all calculations remain basically the same. However, in the first
case, it is evident that
\alpha=0
and
n=0
because the geometry
has only cusps and no dendrites and we refer to Remarks
2.4

and
2.8
.

In what follows, we use that the distribution of balls is mutually
independent. That means, given a ball around
x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
,
the set
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}\backslash\left\{x_{i}\right\}
is also a Poisson
process. W.l.o.g. , we assume
x_{i}=x_{0}=0
with
B_{0}:=\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(0\right)}
.
First we note that
p\in\partial B_{0}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
if and only
if
p\in\partial B_{0}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, which holds with probability

\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(p\right)}\right)}=\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(B_{0}\right)}
. This is a fixed quantity,
independent from
p
.

Now assuming
p\in\partial B_{0}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
, the distance to the
closest ball besides
B_{0}
is denoted

r(p)=\mathrm{dist}(p,\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\backslash\partial B_{0})

with a probability distribution

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{dist}}(r):=\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1+r}{\left(p\right)}\right)}/\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(p\right)}\right)}\,.

It is important to observe that
\partial B_{0}
is
r
-regular
in the sense of Lemma
2.24
. Another important
feature in view of Lemma
3.2
is

r(p)<\Delta(p)
. In particular,
\delta(p)>\frac{1}{2}r(p)
and

\partial B_{0}
is
(\delta,1)
-regular in case
\delta<\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Hence, in what follows, we will derive estimates on
r^{-\gamma}
,
which immediately imply estimates on
\delta^{-\gamma}
.

Estimate on
\gamma
:
A lower estimate for the distribution
of
r(p)
is given by

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{dist}}(r):=\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1+r}{\left(p\right)}\right)}/\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(p\right)}\right)}\approx 1-\lambda\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|r\,.

(6.9)

This implies that almost surely for
\gamma<1

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{(2n)^{d}}\int_{(-n,n)^{d}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}r(p)^{-\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(p)<\infty\,,

i.e.
\mathbb{E}{\left(\delta^{-\gamma}\right)}<\infty
.

Intersecting balls:
Now assume there exists
x_{i}
,
i\neq 0

such that
p\in\partial B_{i}\cap\partial B_{0}
. W.l.o.g. assume

x_{i}=x_{1}:=(2x,0,\dots,0)
and
p=\left(\sqrt{1-x^{2}},0,\dots,0\right)
.
Then

\delta(p)\leq\delta_{0}(p):=2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}

and
p
is at least
M(p)=\frac{x}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}
-regular. Again,
a lower estimate for the probability of
r
is given by (
6.9
)
on the interval
(0,\delta_{0})
. Above this value, the probability
is approximately given by
\lambda\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|\delta_{0}

(for small
\delta_{0}
i.e.
x\approx 1
). We introduce as a new
variable
\xi=1-x
and obtain from
1-x^{2}=\xi(1+x)
that

\delta_{0}\leq C\xi^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad M(p)\leq C\xi^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(6.10)

No touching:
At this point, we observe that
M
is almost
surely locally finite. Otherwise, we would have
x=1
and for every

\varepsilon>0
we had
x_{1}\in\mathbb{B}_{2+\varepsilon}{\left(x_{0}\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{2-\varepsilon}{\left(x_{0}\right)}
.
But

\mathbb{P}_{0}{\left(\mathbb{B}_{2+\varepsilon}{\left(x_{0}\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{2-\varepsilon}{\left(x_{0}\right)}\right)}\approx 1-\lambda 2\left|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right|\varepsilon\;\to\;1\qquad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0\,.

Therefore, the probability that two balls “touch” (i.e. that
x=1
)
is zero. The almost sure local boundedness of
M
now follows from
the countable number of balls.

Extension to
\tilde{\delta}
:
We again study each ball
separately. Let
p\in\partial B_{0}\backslash\overline{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}
with
tangent space
T_{p}
and normal space
N_{p}
. Let
x\in N_{p}

and
\tilde{p}\in\partial B_{0}
such that
x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(\tilde{p})}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}
,
then also
p\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta(\tilde{p})}{\left(\tilde{p}\right)}
and

\delta(p)\in(\frac{7}{8},\frac{7}{6})\delta(\tilde{p})
and
\delta(\tilde{p})\in(\frac{7}{8},\frac{7}{6})\delta(p)

by Lemma
2.24
. Defining

\tilde{\delta}_{i}{\left(x\right)}:=\min\left\{\delta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\,:\;\tilde{x}\in\partial B_{i}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\,\text{s.t. }x\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\delta{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}}{\left(\tilde{x}\right)}\right\}\,,

we find

\tilde{\delta}^{-\gamma}\leq\sum_{i}\chi_{\tilde{\delta}_{i}>0}\tilde{\delta}_{i}^{-\gamma}\,.

Studying
\delta_{0}
on
\partial B_{0}
we can assume
M\leq M_{0}

in (
3.8
) and we find

\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\chi_{\tilde{\delta}_{0}>0}\tilde{\delta}_{0}^{-\gamma-1}\leq C\int_{\partial B_{0}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\delta^{-\gamma}\,.

Hence we find

\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\tilde{\delta}^{-\gamma-1}\leq\sum_{i}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\chi_{\tilde{\delta}_{i}>0}\tilde{\delta}_{i}^{-\gamma-1}\leq\sum_{i}C\int_{\partial B_{i}\backslash\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\delta^{-\gamma}\,.

Estimate on
\beta
:
For two points
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}

let
\mathrm{Circ}_{ij}:=\partial B_{i}\cap\partial B_{j}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\tilde{\delta}}{\left(\mathrm{Circ}_{ij}\right)}:=\bigcup_{p\in\mathrm{Circ}_{ij}}\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{8}\tilde{\delta}(p)}{\left(p\right)}
.
For the fixed ball
B_{i}=B_{0}
we write
\mathrm{Circ}_{0j}
and
obtain
\left|\mathrm{Circ}_{0j}\right|\leq C\delta_{0}^{d}
with

\delta_{0}
from (
6.10
). Therefore, we find

\int_{\mathrm{Circ}_{0j}}(1+M(p))^{\beta}\leq\delta_{0}^{d}(1+M(p))^{\beta}\leq C\xi^{-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-d)}\,.

We now derive an estimate for
\mathbb{E}{\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1+\mathfrak{r}}{\left(0\right)}}\tilde{M}^{\beta}\right)}.

To this aim, let
q\in(0,1)
. Then
x\in\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k+1}}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k}}{\left(0\right)}

implies
\xi\geq q^{k+1}
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1+\mathfrak{r}}{\left(0\right)}}\tilde{M}^{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq C+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k+1}}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k}}{\left(0\right)}}\int_{\mathrm{Circ}_{0j}}(1+M(p))^{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq C+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k+1}}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k}}{\left(0\right)}}C\left(q^{k+1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-d)}

The only random quantity in the latter expression is
\#\left\{x_{j}\in\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k+1}}{\left(0\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{2-q^{k}}{\left(0\right)}\right\}
.
Therefore, we obtain with
\mathbb{E}{\left(\mathbb{X}(A)\right)}=\lambda\left|A\right|
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1+\mathfrak{r}}{\left(0\right)}}\tilde{M}^{\beta}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(q^{k}-q^{k+1}\right)\left(q^{k+1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-d)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(q^{k}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-d-2)}\right)\,.

Since the point process has finite intensity, this property carries
over to the whole ball process and we obtain the condition
\beta<d+2

in order for the right hand side to remain bounded.

Estimate on
\hat{d}
:
We have to estimate the local maximum
number of
A_{3,k}
overlapping in a single point in terms of
\tilde{M}
.
We first recall that
\hat{\rho}(p)\approx 8\tilde{M}(p)\tilde{\rho}(p)
.
Thus large discrepancy between
\hat{\rho}
and
\tilde{\rho}
occurs
in points where
\tilde{M}
is large. This is at the intersection
of at least two balls. Despite these “cusps”, the set
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

consists locally on the order of
\hat{\rho}
of almost flat parts.
Arguing like in Lemma
4.4
resp.
Remark
4.5
this yields
\hat{d}\leq d-1
.
∎

It remains to verify bounded average connectivity of the Boolean set

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}
or its complement. Associated with the connected component

\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}
there is a graph distance

\forall x,y\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}\quad d(x,y):=\inf\left\{l(\gamma):\,\gamma\text{ path in }\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}\text{ from }x\text{ to }y\right\}\,.

Using this distance, we shall rely on the following concept.

Definition 6.10

(Statistical Strech Factor)
.

For
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}
and
R>\mathfrak{r}
we denote

S(x,R):=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}\cap\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(x\right)}\end{subarray}}\frac{d(x,y)}{R}\,,\qquad S(x):=\sup_{R>\mathfrak{r}}S(x,R)\,,

the statistical local strech factor
S(x,R)
and statistical (global)
strech factor
S(x)
.

Lemma 6.11
.

There exists
S_{0}>1
depending
only on
d
and
\lambda
such that for
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}

it holds

\forall S>S_{0}:\qquad\mathbb{P}{\left(S(x)>S\right)}\leq\frac{2\mu}{\nu}e^{-\frac{\nu}{2\mu}S}\,.

In order to prove this, we will need the following large deviation
result.

Theorem 6.12

(Shape Theorem
[
29
, Thm 2.2]
)
.

Let
\lambda>\lambda_{c}
. Then there
exist positive contants
\mu
,
\nu
and
k_{0}
such that the
following holds: For every
k>k_{0}

\mathbb{P}{\left(S(0,k)>\mu\right)}\leq e^{-\nu k}\,.

Proof of Lemma
6.11
.

We have

\displaystyle S(0,k)

\displaystyle>\alpha\mu

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\exists x,y\in\mathbb{B}_{k}{\left(0\right)}:\quad d(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\mu k\,,

\displaystyle S(0,\alpha k)

\displaystyle>\mu

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\exists x,y\in\mathbb{B}_{\alpha k}{\left(0\right)}:\quad d(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\mu k\,,

i.e.

\mathbb{P}{\left(S(0,k)>\alpha\mu\right)}\leq\mathbb{P}{\left(S(0,\alpha k)>\mu\right)}\leq e^{-\frac{\nu}{\mu}(\alpha\mu)k}\,.

One quickly verifies for
k\in\mathbb{N}
that
S(0,k)\leq S
and
S(0,k+1)\leq S

implies
S(0,k+r)\leq 2S
for all
r\in(0,1)
. Hence we find

\mathbb{P}{\left(S(x)>S\right)}\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}{\left(S(0,k)>\frac{S}{2}\right)}\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}e^{-\frac{\nu}{2\mu}Sk}\leq\frac{2\mu}{\nu}e^{-\frac{\nu}{2\mu}S}\,.

∎

While the choice of the points
\left(p_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

is clearly specified in Section
4.1
, there
is lots of room in the choice and construction of
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}
. In
what follows, we choose
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}
in the form (
2.51
).
Then we find the following:

Theorem 6.13
.

Under the above assumptions on the construction
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}
, as well as
p>d
and using the notation of Lemma

5.2
, for every
1\leq r<s<p
there almost surely exists

C>0
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every
u\in W_{0,\partial(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}^{1,p}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\left(\frac{1}{\left|n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\sum_{k:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{k}>0}\sum_{j:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{j}<0}\frac{d_{j}^{-\tilde{\alpha}\frac{s}{r}}\left|\partial_{l}\Phi_{j}\right|}{D_{l+}^{\Phi}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{k}u-\mathcal{M}_{j}u\right|^{s}\right)^{\frac{r}{s}}+F_{s}^{3}(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}},u)\\
\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,,

and for every
u\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}_{0,\partial(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}^{1,p}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})

\left(\frac{1}{\left|n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\sum_{k:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{k}>0}\sum_{j:\,\partial_{l}\Phi_{j}<0}\frac{d_{j}^{-\tilde{\alpha}\frac{s}{r}}\left|\partial_{l}\Phi_{j}\right|}{D_{l+}^{\Phi}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\right)^{\frac{r}{s}}+F_{s}^{3,\mathfrak{s}}(n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}},u)\\
\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}\cap n\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\,.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
be a Poisson point
process with finite intensity. Generate a Voronoi tessellation from

\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
and for each
x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
let
d_{a}
be the
diameter of the corresponding Voronoi cell. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

the following function has finite expectation

f_{n}:=\sum_{a}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{nd_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\,.

Note that this statement is not covered by Lemma
3.18

due to the lack of a minimal distance between the points.

Proof.

Given the condition
0\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
we observe

\mathbb{E}{\left(\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{nd_{0}}{\left(0\right)}}\right)}(x)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}{\left(d_{0}\in[k,k+1)\right)}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{k+1}{\left(0\right)}}(x)\,.

Since
\mathbb{P}{\left(d_{0}\in[k,k+1)\right)}\leq e^{-\alpha k}
for some
\alpha>0
,
we infer

\mathbb{E}{\left(\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{nd_{0}}{\left(0\right)}}\right)}\,(x)\leq Ce^{-\alpha\left|x\right|}\,.

From here, we conclude with the exponentially in
N
decreasing probability
to find more than
N
points within
[0,1]^{d}
:

\mathbb{E}{\left(\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}\cap[0,1]^{d}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{nd_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\right)}(x)\leq Ce^{-\beta\left|x\right|}\,,

for some
\beta>0
. Summing up over all cubes we infer

\mathbb{E}(f_{n})(0)\leq C\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-\beta\left|x-k\right|}\leq C\sum_{N\in\mathbb{N}}N^{d-1}e^{-\beta N}<\infty\,.

∎

Similar to the proof of Theorem
6.5
it will
be necessary to introduce the following quantity for
y\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}

based on (
2.27
):

\mathcal{M}_{y}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x):=\overline{\nabla_{y,\mathfrak{r}}^{\bot}}u{\left(x-y\right)}+\fint_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(y\right)}}u\,.

An important property of
\mathcal{M}_{y}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is the following.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}

with
\left|y_{1}-y_{2}\right|<2
and
\delta:=\frac{1}{2}\sup\left\{r:\,\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(y_{1}+y_{2}\right)\right)}\subset\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\right\}
.
Then there exists
f:\,\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(\left\{y_{1},y_{2}\right\}\right)}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)-\mathcal{M}_{y_{2}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(\left\{y_{1},y_{2}\right\}\right)}}^{s}}^{s}\,,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{1}}u(x)-\mathcal{M}_{y_{2}}u(x)\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(\left\{y_{1},y_{2}\right\}\right)}}^{s}}^{s}\,,

and

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(\left\{y_{1},y_{2}\right\}\right)}}\left|f\right|^{\frac{sp}{p-s}}\leq C\delta^{\frac{s(p-d)}{p-s}-1}\,.

(6.11)

Furthermore for some fixed
C>0
and for every
y\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y\right)}}\sum_{i}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{i}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}+\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y}^{\mathfrak{s}}\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y\right)})}^{s}\,.

(6.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y\right)}}\sum_{i}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{i}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}}\left|\tau_{i}u-\mathcal{M}_{y}u\right|^{s}+\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{a}u-\mathcal{M}_{y}\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y\right)})}^{s}\,.

(6.13)

Proof.

We only treat the vector valued case, the other is proved similarly
using results from Section
2.4
.
W.l.o.g let
y_{1}=y\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}
and
y_{2}=-y\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}
. Let
n=\min\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\,\mathbb{B}_{2^{-n}\mathfrak{r}}{\left(0\right)}\subset\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}}\right\}
,
i.e.
\delta\in(2^{-n-1}\mathfrak{r},2^{-n}\mathfrak{r})
. Furthermore, let
\alpha_{k}:=2\mathfrak{r}\sum_{j=1}^{k}2^{-(n-j)}

for
k=1,\dots,n
and
\alpha_{-k}=-\alpha_{k}
with
\alpha_{0}=0
.
Using (
2.27
), for every number
j=-n,\dots,n

let further

\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\mathfrak{s}}u:=\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-\left|j\right|\right)}}\,.

Then for
j\geq 0
we find from Lemma
2.18

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)-\mathcal{M}_{j+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}u(x)\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j\right)}}u(x)-\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j\right)}}u(x)\right|^{s}\right.+

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\left|\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j\right)}}u(x)-\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j-1\right)}}u(x)\right|^{s}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\mathfrak{r}2^{-n})^{s-d}2^{j(s-d)}\left\|\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L^{s}{\left(\mathrm{conv}\left(\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j\right)}}{\left(\left\{\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1},\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right\}\right)}\right)\cup\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j-1\right)}}{\left(\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right)}\right)}}^{s}

Defining

\tilde{f}^{s}:=\sum_{j}(\mathfrak{r}2^{-n})^{s-d}2^{j(s-d)}\chi_{\mathrm{conv}\left(\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j\right)}}{\left(\left\{\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1},\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right\}\right)}\right)\cup\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j-1\right)}}{\left(\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right)}}

and using local finiteness of the covering as well as

\left|\mathrm{conv}\left(\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j\right)}}{\left(\left\{\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1},\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right\}\right)}\right)\cup\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}2^{-\left(n-j-1\right)}}{\left(\alpha_{j+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\right)}\right|\leq C\left(\mathfrak{r}^{d}2^{-d(n-j)}\right)\,,

we find with
\frac{\left(s-d\right)p}{p-s}+d=\frac{s(p-d)}{p-s}-1=\frac{s(1-d)-p+s}{p-s}=\frac{2s-d-p}{p-s}

\delta

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(\left\{y_{1},y_{2}\right\}\right)}}\left|\tilde{f}\right|^{\frac{sp}{p-s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j}(\mathfrak{r}2^{-n})^{\frac{\left(s-d\right)p}{p-s}}2^{j\frac{\left(s-d\right)p}{p-s}}\mathfrak{r}^{d}2^{-d(n-j)}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta^{\frac{s(p-d)}{p-s}}\sum_{j=1}^{\ln_{2}\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\delta}}2^{j\frac{s(1-d)}{p-s}}\leq C\delta^{\frac{s(p-d)}{p-s}}\delta^{-1}

From here we conclude the first part. Inequality (
6.12
)
follows from the fact that
\tilde{\rho}_{i}\propto\mathfrak{r}_{i}
and the
disjointness of the balls
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}
with
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}

and Lemma
2.17
with
\mathfrak{r}=const
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.13
.

We work with the enumeration
\left(p_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathbb{N}}

and make use of the underlying point process
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
: For every

a\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
y_{x_{a}}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}
such that
x_{a}\in\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y_{x_{a}}\right)}

for every
p_{i}
there almost surely exists a unique
y_{p_{i}}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois}}

such that
p_{i}\in\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y_{p_{i}}\right)}
. Due to the minimal mutual
distance of points in
\mathbb{X}_{\mathfrak{r}}
, we can conclude the following:
Since
p_{i}\in\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(G_{a}\right)}
,
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}\subset\tilde{P}\cap G_{a}

there exists a constant
C
depending only on
\mathfrak{r}
and
d
such
that always

\left|y_{p_{i}}-y_{x_{a}}\right|\leq C\,d_{a}\,.

(6.14)

Since

\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\leq 3\left(\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{p_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}+\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{x_{a}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}+\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{x_{a}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{p_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\right)

we find

\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}f_{a}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}\leq I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}

where we provide and estimate
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
as
follows: First, we observe there exists
n_{0}
such that
n_{0}\mathfrak{r}>1
.
Then with help of (
6.12
)

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}f_{a}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{p_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}f_{a}\sum_{y_{b}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}}\sum_{p_{i}\in\partial\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y_{b}\right)}}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{b}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{2d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\sum_{y_{b}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}}\sum_{p_{i}\in\partial\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y_{b}\right)}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{\rho}_{i}}{\left(p_{i}\right)}}\left|\tau_{i}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{b}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\left(\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{2d_{a}}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}\sum_{y_{b}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y_{b}\right)}}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)^{\frac{p-s}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{s}{p}}\,.

Because of Lemmas
3.18
and
6.14

and the exponential decay of probabilities of
d_{a}
the first integral
on the right hand side is always bounded. Note that (
6.12
)
also implies

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}f_{a}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{x_{a}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}f_{a}\,d_{a}^{d}\,\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{x_{a}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\left(\sum_{y_{b}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})\cap\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y_{b}\right)}}d_{a}^{2d}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)^{\frac{p-s}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{s}{p}}\,.

Again, the first integral on the right hand side is bounded.

Last, the term

I_{3}:=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}f_{a}\frac{\left|\partial_{l}\phi_{i}\right|}{D_{l+}}\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{x_{a}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{p_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

is the most tricky part.

We find a path
Y(y_{x_{a}},y_{p_{i}})=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n(x_{a},p_{i})})

such that
y_{1}=y_{x_{a}}
,
y_{n(x_{a},p_{i})}=y_{p_{i}}
such
that
y_{j}
,
y_{j+1}
are neighbors. By our assumptions, for every
two points
y,\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}
with
y-\tilde{y}<2\mathfrak{r}
,
the convex hull of
\mathbb{B}_{\mathfrak{r}}{\left(\left\{y,\tilde{y}\right\}\right)}
lies
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}_{\infty}
. Hence we iteratively replace sequences
(\dots y_{i},y_{i+1},y_{i+2},\dots)

in the path
Y
by
(\dots y_{i},y_{i+2},\dots)
if
\left|y_{i+2}-y_{i}\right|<2\mathfrak{r}
.
Hence, w.l.o.g we obtain from (
6.14
)
and the definition of the statistical strech factor

n(x_{a},p_{i})\leq 2\frac{\mathrm{Length}Y}{\mathfrak{r}}\leq 2\mathfrak{r}^{-1}Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})\,.

Therefore, for
y\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}
with
\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(y\right)}}\chi_{G_{a}}\neq 0

we observe and the shortest path
Y(x_{a},y_{p_{i}})
and with Lemma

6.15

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{x_{a}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{p_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\mathfrak{r}^{-1}Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})\right)^{s}\sum_{k=1}^{n(x_{a},y_{p_{i}})-1}\left|\mathcal{M}_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u-\mathcal{M}_{y_{k+1}}^{\mathfrak{s}}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\mathfrak{r}^{-1}Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})\right)^{s}\sum_{k=1}^{n(x_{a},y_{p_{i}})-1}\left\|f\nabla^{s}u\right\|_{L_{\mathbb{B}_{1}{\left(\left\{y_{k},y_{k+1}\right\}\right)}}^{s}}^{s}\,.

Now all points
y_{i}\in Y(x_{a},y_{p_{i}})
lie within a radius
of
2Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})
around
x_{a}
, wich implies

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{2Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}d_{a}^{d}f^{s}\left|\nabla^{s}u\right|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\left(\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{2Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}d_{a}^{d}f^{s}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\right)^{\frac{p-s}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right|}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{s}{p}}\,.

Now, by independence of the respective variables, the constant in
front converges to

\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{x_{a}\in\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}\chi_{\mathbb{B}_{2Cd_{a}S(y_{x_{a}})}{\left(x_{a}\right)}}d_{a}^{d}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-s}}\,\mathbb{E}f^{\frac{ps}{p-s}}\right)^{\frac{p-s}{p}}\,.

The first term in the product can be estimated with help of Lemma

3.18
and is bounded for every
p
and
s
by
the exponential distribution of
d_{a}
and
S
. The second term
can be estimated similarly.
∎

A further important property which we will not use in this work,
but which is central for part III of this series is the following
result.

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty,\mathfrak{r}}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}:\,\,\forall y\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty}\backslash\{x\}\,\left|x-y\right|>\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{8}\right\}

be a Matern reduction of the infinite component. For fixed
y_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty,\mathfrak{r}}

and every
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty,\mathfrak{r}}
let
P(y_{0},\tilde{y})=\left(y_{0},y_{1}(\tilde{y}),\dots,y_{N}(\tilde{y})\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

with
y_{N}(\tilde{y})=\tilde{y}
be the shortest path of points
in
\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty,\mathfrak{r}}
connecting
y_{0}
and
\tilde{y}
in

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}
and having length
L(y_{0},\tilde{y})
. Then there exists

\displaystyle\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}:\,[0,L(y_{0},\tilde{y})]\times\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle(t,z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}(t,z)

such that
\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}(t,\cdot)
is invertible for every

t
and
\left\|\partial_{t}\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2
.
For
R>1
let

N_{y_{0},R}(x):=\#\left\{\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(y_{0}\right)}\cap\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{mat}}:\,\exists t:\,x\in\gamma_{y_{0},\tilde{y}}{\left(t,\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(0\right)}\right)}\right\}\,.

Then for every
y_{0}
there exists almost surely
C>0
,
S>0

such that it holds

N_{y_{0},R}(x)\leq C\left(R^{d}-\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{d}\right)\quad\text{for }\left|x-y_{0}\right|<SR\,,\qquad N_{y_{0},R}(x)=0\quad\text{else.}

Proof.

The function
\gamma_{y_{0,}\tilde{y}}
consists basically of pipes
connecting
y_{i}(\tilde{y})
with
y_{i+1}(\tilde{y})
that conically
become smaller within the ball
\mathbb{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\left(y_{i}(\tilde{y})\right)}

to fit through the connection between two neighboring balls. Defining

N_{y_{0},r,R}(x):=\#\left\{\tilde{y}\in{\left(\mathbb{B}_{R}{\left(y_{0}\right)}\backslash\mathbb{B}_{r}{\left(y_{0}\right)}\right)}\cap\mathbb{X}_{\mathrm{pois},\infty,\mathfrak{r}}:\,\exists t:\,x\in\gamma_{y_{0},\tilde{y}}{\left(t,\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{16}}{\left(0\right)}\right)}\right\}

we apply Lemma
6.11
instead of
[
28
]

implies
N_{y_{0},r,R}(x)=0
for all
\left|x-y_{0}\right|>SR
but
also due to the minimal mutual distance
N_{y_{0},r,R}(x)\leq CR^{d-1}(SR-r)
,
where
C
depends only on
\mathfrak{r}
and
d
. From here we follow the
proof of Theorem
6.7
.
∎

We close this section and this work by proving Theorem
1.18
.

Proof of Theorem
1.18
.

The statement on the support is provided by Theorem
4.10

and the fact that we restrict to functions with support in
m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
.
Hence in the following we can apply all cited results to
\mathbb{B}_{m^{1-\beta}}{\left(m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}\right)}

instead of
m\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}
. According to Lemmas
4.7

and
5.2
–
5.3
and to Theorem
6.13

we need only need to ensure
p>d
as well as

\mathbb{E}\left(1+M_{[\frac{1}{8}\delta],\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{kr}+\mathbb{E}\left|\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right|^{-\frac{sr}{s-r}}<\infty\,,

where
k=1
for the simple extension case and
k=2
for the symmetric
extension case. Since
d_{a}
is distributed exponentially and the
corresponding terms are bounded as long as
r\neq s\neq p
, we observe
that we do not have to care about the involved polynomial terms.

According to Theorem
6.8
it is sufficient
that
\frac{sr}{s-r}<2
(i.e.
\frac{pr}{p-r}<2
) and
kr<d+2
.
∎
